Today, even the lowliest mechanical arts <term>mechanical arts: manual trades or crafts, such as blacksmithing or weaving, which were historically viewed as less prestigious than the "liberal arts" of the elite.</term> are treated with more respect than Virtue is; for in those trades, no one presumes to judge them unless they actually practice the craft. # TO THE READER

<meta>catchword: dif-</meta>

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'E' featuring foliate patterns and scrolls within a square border.</image-desc>

**E**verything is ready (oh Kind Reader) and I am here with a little salad <note>The author uses "insalatina" (a little salad) as a metaphor for a curated collection of diverse and pleasant topics.</note> of the finest herbs and beautiful flowers produced by the delightful garden of Chemistry <note>original: "Chimica." In 1672, this term encompassed both practical chemical science and the older traditions of alchemy.</note>. Here, certainly, my Guide <note>The author refers to his previously published guidebook or manual.</note> will be favorable to you in many places; therefore, hold it dear. I suppose that you are not without a copy, since from the day I first brought it to light—which was at the end of the year sixteen hundred and seventy-two—fourteen thousand copies have been printed until now. And now, as there are no more copies left here in Modena <note>original: "Modona," the historic spelling of the Italian city.</note>, it is being reprinted; and on this occasion, I will add a dis- <note>The word is cut off and continues on the next page, likely as "discorso" (discourse or treatise).</note>

<summary>In this preface, the author introduces a new edition of his popular chemistry guide, noting that 14,000 copies have been printed since 1672 and explaining that a new printing is necessary due to high demand in Modena.</summary>
<keywords>preface, Chemistry, Modena, publishing history, 17th century, alchemy, book circulation</keywords>

being no further need for
gold, nor silver, nor other
metals where they are present,
since the virtues and properties of
all things are found in
them with the help of the
**Spagyric** <term>Spagyric</term> <note>The "Spagyric art" refers to alchemical medicine, specifically the process of separating and then recombining the essential components of a substance to create a more potent remedy.</note> art;
therefore, oh bene-
volent reader, welcome this
little work, and keep it like
a true treasure, because here
you will find as much as you could
desire in the world for
living with a happy health;
I warn you, that
finding in this little book
some thing that has [a touch]
of the fabulous, as one might
say of **Artephius** <note>Artephius was a legendary medieval alchemist who allegedly discovered the secret of the Philosopher's Stone and was said to have lived for over a thousand years.</note>, which is a thing more-

<summary>The author argues that Antimony and Mercury, through alchemical preparation, can replace all other metals and expensive medicines. He encourages the reader to cherish the book as a treasure for maintaining health, while acknowledging that some claims might seem legendary.</summary>

<keywords>Antimony, Mercury, Spagyric art, alchemy, Artephius, medicinal virtues, health, alchemical metals</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion of the legendary alchemist Artephius, transitioning from a discussion of "fabulous" or legendary claims to the author's personal humility regarding his own alchemical writings.</meta>

more likely apocryphal than canonical;
accept it in whatever sense seems right
to you. And regarding a chart
of my own invention which you
will find at the end of the **Triumph
of Antimony** <note>original: *Trionfo dell' Antimonio* — likely referring to a section of this manuscript or a specific alchemical tradition, often associated with the works of Basil Valentine.</note>
which demonstrates the matter and the
labor of the **Great Work** <term>Great Work</term> <note>The *Magnum Opus*, the ultimate goal of alchemy: the creation of the Philosopher's Stone and the spiritual transformation of the alchemist.</note>,
that is, the **Philosopher's Stone**, do not
believe that I have ever set myself
to such a preparation;
but that is the way, and how I
would do it, if I found myself
worthy to reach the enjoyment of
such a great treasure, having
derived this from the writings of
the most serious Authors who
have written on such matters.
Therefore I have placed it here,
so that if you can derive
some utility from it, give praise to
Almighty God, since from
Him every good thing is received.

<summary>The author admits that his instructions for the "Great Work" are theoretical and based on his research of authoritative texts rather than personal success. He presents a custom alchemical chart for the Philosopher's Stone, modestly attributing any potential benefit to the reader's own worthiness and God's grace.</summary>

<keywords>Triumph of Antimony, Great Work, Philosopher's Stone, alchemical theory, humility, divine grace, alchemical chart</keywords>

Artephius <note>A legendary figure in alchemy, often claimed to have lived for a thousand years by using the "Philosopher's Stone."</note>
Aristotle <note>The ancient Greek philosopher; medieval alchemy relied heavily on works attributed to him, though many were later found to be written by others (pseudo-Aristotle).</note>
Archelaus <note>An early alchemist, often cited as the teacher of Socrates in ancient traditions, though primarily known in this context for alchemical poetry.</note>
The Art of Gold-Making <note>original: "Ars Aurifera"; a highly influential collection of alchemical treatises first published in Basel in 1572.</note>
Actuarius <note>Joannes Actuarius (c. 1275–1328), a prominent Byzantine physician at the court of Constantinople.</note>
Avicenna <note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose medical and philosophical writings dominated European universities for centuries.</note>
The Reverend Father Lord Basilius Valentinus <note>A mythical 15th-century Benedictine monk; the name was a pseudonym for the author(s) of influential 17th-century chemical texts.</note>
Ludovico Buccafurro <note>A 16th-century philosopher and professor of medicine at the University of Bologna.</note>
Bernard Penot <note>Bernard Georges Penot (c. 1519–1617), a French physician and alchemist who spent his life seeking the Philosopher's Stone and corresponded with leading scientists.</note>
The Sound of the Trumpet <note>original: "Clangor Buccinae"; an anonymous and widely cited alchemical text that uses musical metaphors to describe the chemical process.</note>
Johannes Clossaeus <note>A 17th-century physician and editor of Paracelsian medical works.</note>
Balduin Clodius <note>A German chemist and physician known for his practical laboratory experiments and "chymical" medicines in the late 16th century.</note>
Calid <note>Khalid ibn Yazid (died c. 704), an Umayyad prince who is legendary as the first Arab alchemist.</note>
Oswald Croll <note>A prominent follower of Paracelsus who advocated for "iatrochemistry"—the use of chemicals rather than herbs for healing.</note>
Democritus <note>The Greek "laughing philosopher"; alchemists often cited "Pseudo-Democritus" for early recipes involving the dyeing of metals.</note>
Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), whose "On Medical Material" was the foundational text for botany and pharmacology for over 1,500 years.</note>
Dioscorus <note>An ancient commentator on alchemical texts, often associated with the school of Alexandria.</note>

Quercetanus <note>The Latinized name of Joseph Duchesne (c. 1544–1609), a prominent French physician who championed the use of chemical medicines and served as a bridge between Paracelsian theory and mainstream medicine.</note>
Raymond Lull <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan philosopher; while he was not an alchemist himself, a vast body of influential alchemical literature was attributed to him in the centuries following his death.</note>
Richard the Englishman <note>Richardus Anglicus (fl. c. 1180–1230), an influential early medical writer often associated with the School of Salerno and credited with several important anatomical and surgical treatises.</note>
Schröder <note>Johann Schröder (1600–1664), a German physician whose *Pharmacopoeia Medico-Chymica* was one of the most successful and widely used medical reference books of the 17th century.</note>
Sennert <note>Daniel Sennert (1572–1637), a prolific German physician and professor who attempted to synthesize the traditional teachings of Galen with the new chemical theories of Paracelsus.</note>
Theodor Kerckring <note>Theodor Kerckring (1638–1693), a Dutch anatomist and physician famous for his anatomical discoveries and his commentary on the alchemical text *The Triumphal Chariot of Antimony*.</note>
Tiling <note>Likely Matthias Tiling (1634–1685), a German physician known for his detailed botanical and pharmaceutical observations.</note>
Tubal-Cain <note>A biblical figure mentioned in Genesis as the "forger of all instruments of bronze and iron"; in alchemical tradition, he was revered as the first master of metallurgy and the chemical arts.</note>
Zapata <note>Giovanni Battista Zapata (c. 1520–1586), an Italian surgeon whose work *Li Maravigliosi Secreti* (The Marvelous Secrets) offered practical chemical and surgical remedies for the common person.</note>
Zwelfer <note>Joannes Zwelfer (1618–1668), an influential German chemist and physician known for his rigorous revisions and commentaries on pharmaceutical standards.</note>

> It is true, without falsehood, certain, and most true <note>original Latin: "Verum sine mendatio, certum est verissimum"</note>. <meta>Continuing from the previous page, which introduced "Chemical Medicine" seated upon a chariot. The Adept <term>Adept: A master alchemist who has successfully discovered the "Great Secret" of transmutation or the Elixir of Life.</term> will find much here to philosophize upon; however, do not stray from Basil Valentine, for you will truly find all that you could desire. I hope that in this triumph, he will assist me with his Chariot, led by the valiant Theodor Kerckring <note>Theodor Kerckring (1638–1693) was a Dutch physician and anatomist whose Latin commentary on Valentine’s "Triumphal Chariot of Antimony" became a standard reference for chemical medicine.</note>, and thus note very well that which follows.

## Of the origin and nature of Antimony.

->Chapter II.<-

<margin>[Basil Valentine, Prince of Spagyrics, has discovered the best that can be done with Antimony.]</margin>I have already said that among all those who have written of Antimony, the one who deserves the prize of being called Prince above all others for his ingenuity in writing is Basil Valentine <term>Spagyrics: A term coined by Paracelsus referring to the alchemical process of separating and then recombining the essential components of a substance to create potent medicines.</term>. He alone is the one who has discovered all the best that can be revealed about it, providing access to its use to the confusion of some who falsely wear the robes of Physicians (adorned with a little Philosophy understood only by themselves for their own profit). ## Whether Antimony is a **Poison**, and Whether it Should be Used.

### Chapter III.

It is most certain that raw **Antimony** is a **poison**, and not one of the lesser ones among the **minerals**, being composed of certain sulfurous, **arsenical** particles <note>original: *solfuree Arsenicale*; early chemists recognized the toxic presence of arsenic often found in antimony ores.</note>—or as others say, of immature and crude parts that our stomach, being weak, can neither digest nor endure. But for this reason, to think that one must abandon the use of **Antimony** once it has been prepared <note>Refers to "spagyric" preparation, where the substance is purified and its "toxicity" supposedly removed through alchemy.</note> is a matter [worthy of concern] merely to contemplate; for if we must shun all poisonous things, then we must also shun almost all **purgatives**, as we have stated above. We would also have to shun all **minerals** and even **metals** still used in the **pharmacies** <term>Officine: The laboratories or workshops where medicinal preparations were compounded.</term>; and even more so, we would have to shun the famous **Antidote of Andromachus**, that is, **Theriac** <note>original: *Teriaca*; a complex, ancient polypharmacy compound used as a universal cure-all. It was highly regulated and famous for its many ingredients.</note>, because the **Viper**, which contains within itself a lethal poison, is used in its preparation. But if all the aforementioned remedies [are used]...

*...that those things which are in man may be expelled.* <note>original: "in homine sunt expellantur."</note> Let, therefore, the raw and indigestible parts be cooked by a skilled hand with the help of fire, and let the **poison** be separated from the medicine, and the **volatile** from the **fixed** <term>Fixed and Volatile: In alchemy, "fixed" substances resist the heat of fire and remain stable, while "volatile" substances evaporate or transform into vapor easily.</term> parts of **Antimony**, bringing it to the use of Medicine; and then you will see what honors and what benefits will be received from it. Indeed, there will certainly be no wretched diseases that, through its means, are not brought to a cure. From it, every sort of medicine can be made that might be found or possessed within medicine, whether in combination or on its own, through any Vegetable, Animal, or Mineral source—fabricating from it **Emetics** <term>Emetics: Medicines that induce vomiting to clear the stomach.</term>, **Cathartics** <term>Cathartics: Strong substances used to purge or cleanse the bowels.</term>, **Cordials** <term>Cordials: Tonics intended to stimulate the heart and improve the patient's spirits.</term>, **Diuretics** <term>Diuretics: Substances that promote the production of urine.</term>, **Diaphoretics** <term>Diaphoretics: Medicines used to induce sweating, often to "sweat out" a fever.</term> etc., as the aforementioned Author notes on page 132.

*For it happens in one way or another that, through preparation, an **acidic humor** <term>Humor: In this context, a liquid essence or "spirit" extracted from a mineral through chemical means.</term> is drawn out from **Antimony** by means of distillations, like a true and pure vinegar. By another way, a transparent red color is prepared, sweet and flavorful like skimmed honey or sugar. <meta>This page continues the description of the chemical transformations of Antimony, completing a sentence from page 22 regarding the "acidic humor of Olympus."</meta>

...of Olympus <note>original: "olympi." In alchemy, "Olympus" often refers to the top of the distilling apparatus or a metaphorical "heavenly" or "exalted" state of a substance.</note> through **sublimation** <term>Sublimation: The process of heating a solid until it turns into a gas and then cools back into a solid, skipping the liquid phase, used for purification.</term> like a **flying Eagle** <note>The "flying eagle" is a common alchemical metaphor for the volatile substance rising during sublimation.</note>, appearing red, yellow, and white; now, driven downward through **descension** <term>Descension: A distillation process where the substance is forced by heat to flow downward into a receiving vessel.</term>, it produces various colors and preparations; so that also through **reverberation** <term>Reverberation: A process of intense heating in a furnace where the flame is reflected or "reverberated" back onto the substance.</term> a metal is made from it similar to common lead. Likewise, a transparent glass is produced—green <note>original: "virum," likely a transcription error for "viride" (green), as green is a characteristic color of antimony glass.</note>, yellow, white, black, and imbued with other colors: however, it is not advised to use these glasses in medicine unless they have first been tested by the trial of salt. It is also resolved into rare and wonderful oils, which are varied and manifold, some of which are perfected with additions, some by themselves without the mixture of other things; some likewise are taken internally, others are only applied externally to common ulcers and wounds. It supplies so many extractions and is variegated with so many colors that it could weary the **Delphic Apollo** <note>Apollo was the god of medicine and prophecy; "Delphic" refers to his famous oracle at Delphi. The author suggests the mineral's changes are so numerous they would exhaust even a god.</note>, if indeed he wished to reveal to us with his oracles all the mutations of its nature which are brought through the door of fire. From it is made **Quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver: Also known as Mercury; here referring to the "Mercury of Antimony," a purified essence believed to be the life-force of the mineral.</term>, and **Sulphur** is made which burns like common sulfur, so much so that **gunpowder** <note>original: "pulvis pirius."</note> can be prepared from it. True and natural **Salt** is made, and many other things are made from it, which things I will write down in an orderly fashion, explaining first...

## Calcination and Glass of Antimony by Basil.

### Chapter IV.

Take some of the best **Antimony** <term>Antimony: In this period, this usually refers to the mineral stibnite (antimony sulfide), which was a central subject of alchemical study due to its transformative properties.</term> that can be obtained—that is, the kind that has long, shining streaks mixed with a golden color. Let it be reduced to a very fine and impalpable powder, and spread the said powder in a flat, shallow pan, which shall be placed over a **furnace** <note>original: "fornello"</note> with a moderate fire. When the said powder begins to smoke, it should be moved, stirring it here and there continually without ceasing. In the event that the said Antimony, due to too much heat (which often happens), should clump together, let it be allowed to cool, and then once more reduce it to powder <note>The author is describing the process of **calcination**, where the substance is heated to drive off volatile components like sulfur, transforming it into an oxide.</note>.

# Tincture of Antimony, and the refinement of <note>original: "correttione." In alchemy, 'correcting' a substance often meant refining it or removing its toxic properties to make it a safe medicine.</note> Kerkring's glass.

### Chapter VI.

Take some of the aforementioned glass of Antimony and melt it in a <term>crucible|A heat-resistant container used for melting metals or minerals at very high temperatures</term>, leaving it in the fire until a third of it is consumed; after it has cooled, let it be reduced to a very fine and impalpable powder. Place this inside a flask, pouring over it some highly rectified <term>spirit of wine|High-proof alcohol, usually distilled from wine, used as a primary solvent in pharmaceutical preparations</term> so that it covers it by three fingers' depth, and closing the mouth very well, so that it does not "breathe" <note>The text warns against 'breathing' (respiri), meaning the evaporation or loss of volatile spirit through leaky seals.</note> through the joints of the <term>head|The 'alembic head' or 'cap' that sits atop a flask to collect and condense vapors</term>

## Liquor of Sulfur of Antimony

### Chapter IX

Concerning the tincture extracted from vinegar (which is nothing else but the <term>Sulfur of Antimony</term> <note>In alchemy, "Sulfur" refers to the combustible, oily, or "soul" principle of a mineral, thought to contain its essential medicinal power.</note>): after it has been dried and pulverized as I described above, place it into the cavity of an egg white—that is, where the yolk was. Place this inside a dish in a damp location, where it will dissolve into a liquor. Be careful, however, not to break that thin membrane that separates the yolk from the egg white, as the white would absorb all the liquor; therefore, remove the yolk with great dexterity.

The aforementioned liquor heals every wound wonderfully, whether it be a puncture or a cut, if applied immediately by anointing the wound with a <term>quill</term> <note>Italian: penna; a large feather used as a delicate brush for medicinal application.</note>, placing a small cloth soaked in the said liquor over it, and applying around it some...

...purity <note>original: "za", likely concluding "purezza". *This oil is the quintessence* <note>Latin: *quinta essentia*; the "fifth element" or the purest, most concentrated medicinal force extracted from a natural substance.</note>, *which is the highest thing that can be written regarding Antimony, as is also found in my previous writings in which I made a brief declaration of Antimony. <meta>This page continues the text from Basil Valentine's "Triumphal Chariot of Antimony," specifically describing the final processing of the Oil of Antimony and its extraordinary medicinal virtues. It follows a discussion of the four instruments or stages of preparation.</meta>

...the subtle is separated, and after that separation follows **fixation** <term>fixatio; the process of making a volatile substance stable and resistant to heat</term> through the final **coagulation** <term>coagulatio; the solidification of a liquid into a solid mass</term>, by which at last the matter is reduced to a transparent, fiery stone. So that this stone may act upon metals, it must then be fermented due to its penetrative property—though not as much as that true **Philosopher’s Stone** <note>original: "lapis Philosophorum"; the legendary substance sought by alchemists to transform base metals and provide eternal life.</note>, because this is not a "universal" medicine, but only "tints" <note>In alchemy, to "tint" or "tincture" refers to the transformative power to change the color and quality of a substance, often on a specific or "particular" scale rather than a total universal change.</note> particularly. Its dose, before it is coagulated, is eight grains taken with pure wine. It makes a person grow young again and frees them from all melancholy. Furthermore, whatever in the human body receives growth and increase—such as hair and nails—falls away, and the person is renewed just as the **Phoenix** <note>original: "Phænix"; a mythical bird of antiquity said to burn itself on a funeral pyre only to be reborn from the ashes.</note> (if indeed this mythical bird, which I only mention as an example, is actually found anywhere on earth) is renewed by fire. For it consumes all symptoms in the human body like a consuming fire, to which it is rightfully compared; it puts every evil to flight.

...and it expels everything that **potable gold** <term>aurum potabile; a legendary alchemical medicine consisting of gold dissolved in liquid, believed to be a universal panacea</term> can expel.

Thus it is necessary, in order to come to enjoy such a great treasure, to spare no effort in interpreting that particular **dexterity** <note>original: "destrezza"; refers to the specific manual skill or secret technique required for the chemical operation.</note> mentioned by our **Basil** <note>Basil Valentine, the purported 15th-century Benedictine monk and alchemist whose works were published in the early 1600s.</note> for the distillation of the aforementioned oil. Let the devoted student be satisfied that I have told him the time required to **circulate** <term>circulatio; the process of continuous distillation and condensation in a closed vessel to refine a substance</term> the matter, and what sort of vinegar must serve for such an operation. For Basil simply says "**distilled vinegar**" <note>original: "Aceto destillato"</note>, which in its pure sense is nothing other than wine vinegar; however, this is not good for extracting the **tincture of coral** <note>In alchemy, a tincture is a potent extract that carries the essential "soul" or medicinal property of a substance—in this case, coral.</note>, and whoever does not wish to believe this will prove it through experience, as I have done, and will see what results. For the rest—that is, regarding the aforementioned dexterity—the curious reader may consult the most learned **Kerckring** <note>Theodore Kerckring (1638–1693), a Dutch physician and anatomist known for his 1671 commentary on Basil Valentine’s "Triumphal Chariot of Antimony."</note> in his aforementioned Commentary, where with a **Riddle** <note>original: "Enigma"; alchemists often hid their most important secrets in allegories or riddles to prevent the "unworthy" from discovering them.</note> he teaches the method of performing this operation. To make everything easy for the reader, here are his words in the aforementioned Commentary on page 159:

The Riddle of Kerckring
</margin>

# Oil of Ordinary Glass of Antimony

### Chapter 11

Grind the **Glass [of Antimony]** <term>Glass of Antimony; a transparent, deep red or orange glass-like substance produced by roasting and melting antimony ore, used as a powerful medicinal base</term> as finely as possible. Place over it very clear **verjuice** <note>original: "agresto"; the acidic juice of unripe grapes</note> in a flat-bottomed flask, and let it digest for several days. Then, separate all the juice from the dregs. Afterward, add **sugar candy** <note>original: "Zuccaro candido"</note>—dissolved in a little **distilled vinegar**—in the same quantity as the verjuice; that is, in equal parts.

Place the whole mixture in a **retort** <term>Retort; a glass vessel with a long, downward-curving neck used for distilling liquids</term> that has been very well **luted** <note>original: "lutata"; sealed with a paste or clay to make the joints airtight</note> into a furnace. Apply graduated heat at the beginning, then increase it at the end; a **ruby-red oil** will come forth, which shall be enhanced with highly rectified **Spirit of Wine** <term>Spirit of Wine; concentrated alcohol or ethanol</term>.

This Oil is most perfect for **Epiplepsy** <note>original: "Epiplefia"; likely a transcription variant for epilepsy or "falling sickness."</note>.

The dose is from four to six or eight drops in a suitable **vehicle** <note>Vehicle; a carrier liquid like water or wine used to help the patient swallow the medicine</note>. By adding a little rectified **Spirit of Salt** <term>Spirit of Salt; an old name for hydrochloric acid</term> to this Oil and placing it upon the **calx of Gold** <note>original: "calce di Oro"; the powdered oxide of gold produced by chemical treatment</note> prepared as we shall describe hereafter, it will cause the tincture to pass through the **Alembic** <term>Alembic; a traditional two-part distillation apparatus</term>, leav-

# Calcination of Gold according to Basil.

### Chapter XIII.

Gold is **calcined** <term>Calcination; the process of reducing a metal to a fine powder (calx) through heat or chemical action</term> through **amalgamation** <term>Amalgamation; the process of mixing a metal, in this case gold, with mercury to soften it</term> with **mercury**, and the evaporation of said mercury; the method for this is found in my *Guide to Chemistry*. Afterward, it is dissolved in **aqua regia** <term>Aqua Regia; literally "royal water," a powerful mixture of acids capable of dissolving noble metals like gold</term> made from **saltpeter** <term>Saltpeter; potassium nitrate</term> and **sal ammoniac** <term>Sal ammoniac; ammonium chloride</term> in equal parts, and three parts of crushed **flint** <term>Flint; also called firestone, often used in distillations to help distribute heat or act as a catalyst</term>. This water is distilled in a **retort** <term>Retort; a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids</term>, adding only a very small amount of material at a time. 

Take, I say, one part of the aforementioned gold and three parts of the aforementioned water; once the gold is dissolved, remove the water from it by distillation. Then, to the **calx** <term>Calx; the powdery residue remaining after the chemical breakdown of a metal</term> that remains at the bottom of the **vessel** <note>original: "orina"; referring here to a tall, glass laboratory vessel shaped like a urinal</note>, one shall add as much of the aforementioned water as is required to dissolve it. This should be placed in a **water bath** <term>Water bath; also known as a 'bain-marie,' used for gentle, uniform heating</term> to **digest** <term>Digestion; the application of steady, gentle heat over a long period to mature or refine a substance</term>, so that the dregs may be separated from the solution. This solution shall be digested in the bath for eight or nine natural days; afterward, the water shall be extracted again until it reaches the consistency of an **oil** <note>In alchemy, an "oil" refers to a thick, viscous liquid resulting from chemical processes, rather than a fatty substance</note>. Over this oil, the aforementioned water shall be poured, distilling as described above and repeating...

# Fixed Powder of Antimony.

Having extracted the tincture, pour it off by <term>inclination<note>Decanting; the act of tilting the vessel to pour off liquid while leaving the sediment behind.</note></term> and filter it so that it is clean of all dregs. Freed from the dregs, it shall be distilled to separate the Spirit, and there will remain at the bottom a red, <term>fixed<note>original: "fissa"; a substance that has been made stable and no longer evaporates or changes when exposed to heat.</note></term> powder of the greatest virtue.

The dose is half a <term>dram<note>original: "dramma"; a unit of weight equal to 1/8th of an ounce, roughly 3.9 grams.</note></term>, taken three times a day—that is, in the morning, at midday, and in the evening. It expels all clotted blood from the body, and if continued, it safely opens any dangerous internal <term>abscess<note>original: "postema"; a localized collection of pus or a swelling.</note></term>; therefore, it is a specific remedy for dangerous <term>pleurisy<note>original: "pleuritide"; inflammation of the membranes surrounding the lungs, causing sharp chest pain.</note></term> and the like. It radically expels the <term>French disease<note>original: "morbo gallico"; a historical term for syphilis.</note></term>, causes hair to regrow and renew itself, and the man who uses it shall be rejuvenated.

# Oil of Antimony, or the extraction of said substance elucidated by Kerckring,

### Chapter XVII.

Take some mineral <term>antimony<note>A metallic mineral used in early medicine and alchemy, famous for its perceived power to purge the body and its use in creating "essential oils" and tinctures.</note></term>, the best and purest that can be found; pulverize it finely and place it in a glass flask with its own distilled vinegar, so that the liquid rises above it by forty-six <term>fingers<note>original: "deti"; a traditional unit of measurement for the depth of liquid in a vessel, based on the width of a finger. Forty-six fingers is an unusually large amount; it may be a transcription of "four to six" (quattro ò sei).</note></term>. By placing a <term>blind head<note>original: "Capel cieco"; a lid for a distilling vessel that lacks a spout, used to trap vapors and force them to fall back into the liquid, a process called refluxing.</note></term> upon it, let it be placed in <term>digestion<note>The process of providing gentle, continuous heat to a substance to encourage chemical change or extraction.</note></term> for forty days, as this time is required to extract the <term>tincture<note>An essence or concentrated medicinal extract.</note></term>. This tincture will be as red as blood. Once it has been poured off by <term>inclination<note>original: "vota per inclinatione"; the technique of tilting the vessel to pour out the liquid while leaving the solid sediment (dregs) behind.</note></term>, the vinegar shall be drawn off over a slow fire. From the remaining powder, a very deep red tincture shall be extracted once more with the best <term>spirit of wine<note>original: "spirito di Vino"; high-proof distilled alcohol, used as a powerful solvent.</note></term>. This shall be set to <term>circulate<note>To heat a liquid so that it evaporates and then condenses back into the same vessel in a continuous cycle.</note></term> in a <term>pelican<note>original: "Pellicano"; a specialized alchemical vessel with two curved side arms that return condensed vapors to the main body, resembling a pelican's neck.</note></term> with two hollow handles, or else in the <term>"two brothers"<note>original: "duoi fratelli"; a pair of connected glass vessels used for the same purpose as a pelican.</note></term> in a <term>water bath<note>original: "al bagno"; heating a vessel by placing it in a larger container of hot water to ensure even, gentle heat.</note></term>.

# Oil of Antimony Without Addition

### Chapter XXI.

Take the highest quality <term>antimony</term><note>A brittle, silvery-gray metalloid that was a cornerstone of early modern "spagyric" (alchemical) medicine, used for its potent—and often toxic—emetic and purgative effects.</note> and pulverize it very finely, and place it in a flat-bottomed <term>cucurbit</term><note>A gourd-shaped laboratory vessel used in distillation; it served as the base containing the liquid to be heated.</note>, pouring over it <term>vinegar of antimony</term><note>A specialized alchemical solvent. Alchemists believed that minerals contained their own internal "vinegar" or acid which, when extracted, was more powerful for dissolving the parent mineral than ordinary wine vinegar.</note> sharpened with its own salt. Then, having sealed the mouth very well, let it be placed in <term>manure</term><note>original: "lettame"; horse manure was a standard laboratory heat source, providing a constant, gentle, and low temperature through the natural heat of decomposition.</note> and in the <term>bath</term><note>A water bath or "bain-marie," used to heat substances evenly without burning them.</note> to <term>putrefy</term><note>In alchemical terms, putrefaction is the slow breakdown of a substance through warmth and moisture, often seen as the essential first step in releasing its inner virtues.</note> for forty days, or until the "body" is dissolved and the vinegar has become red like blood. 

At that time, the said vinegar is emptied by <term>inclination</term><note>A technique of slowly tilting the vessel to pour off the liquid while leaving the sediment (the "dregs") undisturbed at the bottom.</note>, and more of the new vinegar is poured over the remainder as before. This is repeated until the vinegar no longer takes on any color. Then, all the said vinegars are filtered and, joined together, they are put back into a glass <term>flask</term><note>original: "bozza"; a rounded glass vessel.</note>. Sealing it as before, it shall be put to putrefy as above until it is seen that the body dissolved by the vinegar is perfectly dissolved. This will be signaled by the said matter becoming black like <term>ink</term><note>This refers to the "nigredo" or blackening stage, which alchemists interpreted as a sign of complete material breakdown and the potential for rebirth into a more refined state.</note>. Having taken the said matter, place it in a flask with its

# Another Oil of Antimony

## Oil of Antimony with Addition
->Chap. XXVII.<-

Take one part **Antimony**, another part **Sulfur**, half a part **Sal Ammoniac** <note>Ammonium chloride; a crystalline salt used in alchemy to aid in the sublimation and purification of metals.</note>, and two parts **Quicklime** <note>Calcium oxide; a caustic substance produced by heating limestone.</note>. By repeating this operation three times, the oil will be finished. It is excellent for healing any wound that could not be cured by the power of any other medicine.

# Another Oil of Antimony

# Sulfur of Antimony

### Chapter 32.

One pound of **Antimony**, three or, better yet, four ounces of **Sal Ammoniac** <note>original: "Sal Armoniaco"; ammonium chloride, a salt used frequently in alchemy to facilitate the sublimation of minerals.</note>; let it be sublimated according to the art, and the sublimate be pulverized; adding to it five ounces of more **Antimony**, sublimating it once again as before, and the sublimate shall be washed several times in common water, and once dried one will have the **Sulfur of Antimony**, which burns like ordinary common sulfur.

## Preparation of Antimony for Leprosy.
->Chapter V.<-

Take one pound of Antimony, four pounds of the strongest distilled vinegar, and half a pound of white Tartar <term>Tartaro bianco: Potassium bitartrate, a salty crust that forms in wine barrels; used here as a chemical reagent.</term>. ## Paracelsus’s Flowers of Cheiri.

Paracelsus’s *Cheiri* <note>In Paracelsian alchemy, "Cheiri" refers to the essence or "flower" of a substance, often associated with the color yellow or gold; here it refers specifically to a refined state of antimony.</note> is nothing other than the **Flowers of Antimony** fixed through a long circulation <term>Circolatione: The alchemical process of continuous distillation and condensation in a closed vessel, allowing the substance to "circulate" and refine.</term>; many have described the method, some more and some less tedious, but I want you to enjoy here an easy method found by me. ## Solar Elixir of Antimony, or the Electrum of Paracelsus.

### Chapter VIII.

Take Mercury of Life <term>Mercurio Vita: A white precipitate of antimony, also known as Powder of Algaroth, used medicinally as a potent emetic.</term>, also known as **Algaroth powder**, pure **Tartar** <term>Tartaro: Potassium bitartrate, a salt deposited during the fermentation of wine, used frequently in alchemical preparations as a flux.</term>, and refined **Saltpeter** <term>Salnitro: Potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in many alchemical reactions.</term> in equal parts; let a regulus <term>Regolo: The metallic mass that settles at the bottom of a crucible during the smelting of ores; here, it refers to the purified metallic part of the antimony.</term> be made according to the Art. Take of said regulus and of very fine gold in equal parts; melt them both together inside a crucible <note>original: "Crocio." A heat-resistant ceramic or mineral container used for melting metals at high temperatures.</note>, and once cast and cooled, they shall be reduced to powder. Mixed with four parts of **Sal Ammoniac** <term>Sale Armoniaco: Ammonium chloride, a salt used in alchemy to assist in the sublimation of metals.</term>, let it be sublimated until the **Star of Antimony** <note>original: "Astro dell'Antimonio." In alchemy, this refers to a highly purified form of antimony that often displays a star-like crystalline pattern on its surface.</note> ascends, leaving at the bottom a useless earth, and the

The sublimate <term>Solimato: The solid substance collected after vaporization and condensation; in this context, the purified essence of the mineral.</term> is washed with distilled water to remove the saltiness of the Sal Ammoniac <term>Armoniaco: Ammonium chloride, a salt used in alchemy to aid in the sublimation and cleaning of metals.</term>, and the flowers <term>Fiori: "Flowers" in alchemy refer to the fine, powdery substance that rises during sublimation, representing the volatile essence of the material.</term> will remain at the bottom of the vessel. After these are dry, they shall be placed in the aforementioned vessel to be fixed <term>Fissatione: Fixation is the alchemical process of making a volatile substance stable under heat so that it no longer evaporates or escapes.</term> in the same manner. Care must be taken, both in this and in the previous operation, to manage the fire so that the flowers do not melt or collapse <note>original: "sfondino." Referring to the material losing its airy, crystalline structure and turning back into a mass due to excessive heat.</note> from too much heat. These flowers, which are yellow in color, must first become orange <note>original: "naranzati." From "arancia," meaning orange-colored.</note>, and then a chestnut color, which will be the sign that the fixation is complete. 

Now, take these flowers and extract a tincture <term>Tintura: A concentrated medicinal extract created by dissolving the "spirit" or essence of a substance into a solvent.</term> using alkalized spirit of wine <term>Spirito di vino alchalizato: High-proof alcohol (ethanol) that has been treated with an alkali, such as potassium carbonate, to increase its power as a solvent.</term>, much as was described in the first book. Afterward, the spirit of wine shall be drawn off <note>The alcohol is evaporated away to concentrate the medicine.</note> until the mixture reaches the consistency of an oil. The dose shall be from two to four drops. It is used for every illness where it is necessary to purge the blood, particularly for black jaundice <note>In historical medicine, "black jaundice" was often associated with the gallbladder or a severe imbalance of "black bile."</note>, dropsy <term>Hydropisia: An old medical term for edema, or the swelling of soft tissues due to the accumulation of excess water.</term>, gout, and similar conditions.

And this is as much as I have been able to obtain from **Paracelsus** <note>The influential Swiss-German physician and alchemist (1493–1541) who championed the use of chemicals in medicine rather than just herbs.</note>, though I am certain it is not even a tenth part of what he wrote concerning **Antimony**. Now we shall hear what **Penotus** <note>Bernard Georges Penot (c. 1519–1617), a French physician and alchemist who published extensively on Paracelsian medicine.</note> says in his book on the various preparations of chemical medicines.

# Bernard Penot on Antimony, and its Virtues.

### Chapter IX.

Antimony is believed or esteemed to be a poison by many Doctors of Medicine. And if their opinion were correct, then Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), a Greek physician whose work "De Materia Medica" was the primary botanical and pharmacological text for 1,500 years.</note> did wrong when he assigned it so many times and included it in collyriums <term>Colirij: Eye salves or medicinal washes for the eyes.</term>. Arnaldo of Villanova, Nicolaus, and Myrepsos <note>Arnaldo of Villanova (c. 1240–1311) and Nicolaus Myrepsos (13th century) were influential medieval medical authorities; their inclusion here serves to lend historical weight to the author's argument.</note>—men of such great learning and esteem—also did wrong, for they had it taken as an antidote along with castoreum <term>Castoreo: A substance secreted by beavers, traditionally used in medicine to treat "vapors," hysteria, and nervous disorders.</term> against apoplexy <term>Apoplefia: A historical term for a stroke or sudden loss of consciousness.</term>, paralysis, and vertigo. Antonio Scuneberg <note>Likely referring to Anton von Schonberg, a 16th-century writer on the plague.</note> also did wrong, who placed it in the catalog of medicines against the plague, and says that when taken in great quantity—provided it is prepared for chemical <term>Uso Chimico: "Chemical use" here refers to spagyric or alchemical laboratory preparation, which practitioners believed could "tame" toxic minerals and unlock their healing essences.</term> use—it is excellent. Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a renowned Italian physician and botanist famous for his commentaries on Dioscorides.</note>, who praises it so much, also did wrong. The Bolognese physicians also do wrong, who use it for melancholic diseases <note>Conditions believed to be caused by an excess of "black bile," ranging from chronic sadness to digestive ailments.</note>; for if the aforementioned are prudent and most learned men, and furthermore well-practiced in Medicine, there is no reason to doubt, there- <note>original: "dun-" (catchword for "dunque," meaning "therefore").</note>

The sublimate <term>Solimato: A solid substance produced by the process of sublimation, where a material is heated into a vapor and then collected as it cools back into a solid form.</term> should be washed with distilled water to remove the saltiness of the [sal] ammoniac, and the flowers <term>Fiori (Flowers): In early chemistry, this refers to the fine powder or essence that rises and collects at the top of a vessel during heating.</term> will remain at the bottom of the vessel. Once these are dried, they shall be placed in the vessel mentioned above to be fixed <term>Fissare (Fixation): The process of making a volatile substance stable and resistant to heat.</term> in the same manner. Care must be taken, both in this and in the previous operation, to manage the fire so that the flowers do not melt or collapse due to excessive heat. These flowers, which are yellow in color, must first turn orange, and then the color of a chestnut; this will be the sign that the fixation is complete. 

Now, take these flowers and extract a tincture with alkalized spirit of wine <term>Spirito di vino alchalizato: Highly concentrated alcohol (ethanol) that has been treated with alkaline salts to increase its ability to extract medicinal properties from minerals.</term>, much as was described in the first book. Afterward, the spirit of wine shall be evaporated until it reaches the consistency of an oil. The dose shall be from two to four drops for every illness where it is necessary to purge the blood—particularly for black jaundice <note>original: "itericia nera"</note> <term>Black Jaundice: A historical medical term likely referring to severe liver dysfunction or complications resulting in dark skin discoloration.</term>, for dropsy <term>Hydropisia: The swelling of soft tissues due to excess fluid, known today as edema.</term>, gout, and similar conditions.

And this is all I have been able to obtain from Paracelsus <note>Paracelsus (1493–1541) was a Swiss physician and alchemist who revolutionized medicine by arguing that diseases were caused by external agents and should be treated with chemical remedies rather than just balancing "humors."</note>, though I am certain it is not even a tenth of what he wrote concerning Antimony. Now we shall hear what Penot <note>Bernard Georges Penot (c. 1519–1617) was a French physician and alchemist who spent much of his life defending Paracelsian medicine and refining chemical preparations.</note> says in his book on various preparations of chemical medicines.

# Bernard Penot on Antimony and its Virtues.

### Chapter IX.

By many Doctors of Medicine, Antimony is believed or considered a poison. If their opinion were correct, then Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides was a 1st-century Greek physician and author of the most influential herbal of antiquity.</note> did poorly when he prescribed it so many times and included it in eye-salves <term>Colirij (Collyriums): Ancient medical preparations, usually in the form of liquid washes or ointments, specifically intended for treating the eyes.</term>. Likewise, Arnaldo of Villanova, Nicolaus, and Myrepsos—men of such great learning and esteem—did poorly to have it taken as an antidote alongside castor <term>Castoreo (Castoreum): A yellowish secretion from the scent glands of beavers, used historically in medicine for nervous disorders and as a stimulant.</term> against apoplexy <term>Apoplefia (Apoplexy): An older medical term for a sudden loss of consciousness or paralysis, often what we now call a stroke.</term>, paralysis, and vertigo. Antonio Scuneberg <note>Likely referring to Anton Schneeberger (1530–1581), a German-Swiss physician.</note> also did poorly, as he placed it in the catalog of medicines against the plague, saying that when taken in large quantities and prepared according to chemical use, it is excellent. Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a famous Italian botanist and physician.</note> also did poorly, for he praises it so much. The Bolognese doctors also act poorly when they use it for melancholic diseases <term>Morbi malencolici (Melancholic diseases): In the theory of the four humors, conditions thought to be caused by an excess of black bile, often associated with depression or digestive issues.</term>; yet there is no doubt that these are prudent and most learned men, highly practiced in medicine.

# Bernard Penot on Antimony, and its Virtues.

### Chapter IX.

Antimony is believed or esteemed to be a poison by many Doctors of Medicine, and if their opinion is correct, then Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), an ancient Greek physician whose work "De Materia Medica" was the primary pharmacological text for 1,500 years.</note> did wrong when he assigned it so many virtues and included it in eye-salves <term>Colirij (Collyria): Ancient and medieval medicinal preparations, usually liquid or semi-solid, intended for application to the eye.</term>. <margin>Arnaldo of Villanova and Nicolaus Myrepsus made use of Antimony.</margin> Arnaldo of Villanova and Nicolaus Myrepsus <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311) was a famous physician and alchemist; Nicolaus Myrepsus was a 13th-century Byzantine physician known for his influential medical compendium.</note>—men of such great learning and esteem—also did wrong, for they had it taken as an antidote along with Castoreum <term>Castoreo (Castoreum): A yellowish secretion from the scent glands of the beaver, used historically in medicine to treat nervous conditions and skin ailments.</term> against apoplexy <term>Apoplefia (Apoplexy): A historical term for a stroke or sudden loss of consciousness caused by a hemorrhage or blockage in the brain.</term>, paralysis, and vertigo. They also did wrong, <margin>Anton Schneberger. Mattioli.</margin> specifically Anton Schneberger <note>Anton Schneberger (1530–1581) was a Swiss-born physician who practiced in Poland and wrote on the plague.</note>, who placed it in the catalog of medicines against the plague, and says that when taken in small quantities, having been prepared for chemical use, it is excellent; and Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a renowned Italian doctor and botanist best known for his commentaries on Dioscorides.</note>, who praises it so much. Therefore, if they have used it, what is there to fear? It is then those aforementioned who do wrong by opposing such celebrated men. 

<margin>Mattioli</margin> Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a famous Italian physician and botanist best known for his commentary on Dioscorides.</note> says that it is effective against the poison of the plague, and that it also drives away tertian, quartan, and daily fevers <note>These terms refer to different cycles of malarial fevers: tertian (every 48 hours), quartan (every 72 hours), and daily (quotidian).</note>, and that it purges and cleanses the stomach of every bad humor <term>humor: According to the medical theory of the time, the body was governed by four fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile); an imbalance or "bad humor" was thought to cause disease.</term>. Therefore, by the authority of those mentioned, I will say that by driving out poison, it is a health-bringing remedy and not a poison. 

And furthermore, what remedies act so promptly and so successfully, and heal with such admirable speed, as do its flowers <term>flowers: In alchemy, "flowers" refers to a substance that has been sublimated—heated until it turns to vapor and then condensed back into a fine powder.</term>, oils, tinctures, and other things made from it? <margin>Paracelsus</margin> <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), known as Paracelsus, was a Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of minerals and chemicals in medicine.</note>

The dose of antimony, therefore, is this: at the beginning of an onset of the falling sickness <term>falling sickness: a common historical name for epilepsy.</term>, one should give nine grains; after the paroxysm <term>paroxysm: a sudden violent attack or seizure.</term> or the agitation, give eight or ten grains with a certain diligence; and if the paroxysm returns after an hour, give another nine grains as before, and repeat the same if the illness strikes a third time.

Having taken three grains of the same, and drunk it with oil of sulfur and salt, in a few days it drives out the waters that are between the skin <note>This refers to edema or dropsy, a condition where fluid collects in the body's tissues.</note>. Antimony, in particular, provokes sweating and cures both internal and external ailments; it modifies the blood in every vein, leaves nothing harmful to man, and preserves the natural humor <term>humor: in historical medicine, these were the four vital fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed to govern health and temperament.</term>.

# The Most Learned **Quercetanus** <note>Joseph Duchesne (c. 1544–1609), a French physician and chemist who championed the use of chemical medicines in the Renaissance.</note> on Antimony, in the *Tetras* <note>Refers to his work *Tetrade des plus graves maladies* (The Tetrad of the Most Grave Diseases), published in the late 16th century.</note> at Chapter 31, page 383.

The envy and treacherous rage of certain critics—or rather, their most dense and ugly ignorance—causes them to mumble absurdly about things they do not know, particularly about **Antimony** <term>Antimony: a metallic element that was a centerpiece of "chemical" medicine. We shall therefore teach its various preparations and its most excellent **diaphoretic** <note>Substances that induce sweating, used in early medicine to expel "corrupt humors" or toxins from the body.</note> virtues, which produce more miraculous effects than any other sudorific made from **Holy Wood** <note>Guaiacum, a medicinal wood from the Americas used extensively in the 16th and 17th centuries to treat syphilis and various fevers.</note> or other similar things. ## The Pancrestos Antidote
<note>The name "Pancrestos" comes from the Greek *pankhrestos*, meaning "useful for everything" or a universal remedy.</note>

### Chapter XIII.

Take the red **flowers of the regulus of Mars** <term>flowers of the regulus of Mars<gloss>the sublimation of antimony that has been purified using iron (Mars), resulting in a potent metallic essence</gloss></term>, and place them inside the vessel called the **hell vessel** <note>original: "vaso inferno," likely a specific type of furnace or high-heat container used for intense calcination.</note>. It must be very well **luted** <term>luted<gloss>sealed with airtight clay or cement to prevent the escape of vapors</gloss></term> with the strongest seal so that it does not "breathe." Set it to reverberate for three or four days in a **reverberatory fire** <term>reverberatory fire<gloss>a furnace design where the flame is deflected or "reverberated" off the roof onto the material, creating intense heat without direct contact with fuel</gloss></term>. Once done, a tincture shall be extracted with **radical vinegar** <term>radical vinegar<gloss>a highly concentrated, distilled acetic acid, often prepared from verdigris, used as a powerful solvent in alchemy</gloss></term> (as will be described hereafter) until the vinegar...

Once the tincture has been extracted anew, the aforementioned Spirit is separated until the tincture remains in the form of an oil.

By administering a few drops of this in broth, or in a suitable liquor, it cleanses the entire **mass of the blood** <note>original: "massa sanguinaria," referring to the total volume of blood in the body according to humoral theory.</note>. It restores and increases the **natural heat** <term>natural heat<gloss>the innate bodily warmth that was considered the engine of life and digestion in traditional medicine</gloss></term>, consumes every bad excrement of the body and every viscosity of the **stomach** <note>original: "ventricolo," referring to the stomach or digestive chamber.</note>. For **colic pains** it is a most singular medicine, as it also is for **suffocations of the womb** <note>original: "soffocazioni dell'utero," a historical diagnosis for various physical and emotional distresses in women, often called hysteria.</note>.

If, in place of the **regulus of Mars** <term>regulus of Mars<gloss>metallic antimony that has been purified using iron (Mars)</gloss></term>, one substitutes the **regulus of Jupiter** <term>regulus of Jupiter<gloss>metallic antimony purified using tin (Jupiter)</gloss></term> to prepare the aforementioned **flowers** <note>original: "fiori," the powdery substance created through sublimation.</note>, one can still extract the spirit of wine until dryness. Upon the remaining powder, distilled water shall be distilled several times to **sweeten** <note>original: "dolcificarla," the process of washing out salts or harsh impurities with water.</note> it; once dried, its dose will be three or four grains.

### Chapter XVI

Take the **regulus of Mars** <term>regulus of Mars<gloss>a metallic form of antimony purified using iron</gloss></term>, or **regulus of Venus** <term>regulus of Venus<gloss>antimony purified using copper</gloss></term>, calcined according to the art, and dissolve as much as possible in **Spirit of Salt** <term>spirit of salt<gloss>a historical name for hydrochloric acid</gloss></term>, a fluxing salt <note>original: "gemma flussibile," likely referring to rock salt or sal-gem used to aid the melting or "flow" of minerals</note>. After these have been putrefied and digested for several days, let it be distilled through a **retort** <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids</note> with a very violent, graduated fire. This will yield a thick oil; by rectifying <note>refining through repeated distillation</note> it two or three times (separating the dregs from the pure liquor), it will become subtle and thin. Afterward, let it be circulated with the finest **spirit of wine** <term>spirit of wine<gloss>high-proof alcohol or ethanol</gloss></term> and distilled three or four times, separating the dregs at each distillation. The liquor should then be kept in a closed glass vessel as a most precious treasure. 

Taking two or three drops of this is miraculous for every infirmity, altering and evacuating the body better than a decoction made of a hundred kinds of herbs, and even better than **rhubarb** <note>original: "reubarbaro," a plant root highly valued in historical medicine as a potent purgative</note> taken in a potion. In this way, it purifies the blood, awakens and incites the appetite, and uproots **cachexia** <term>cachexia<gloss>a state of physical wasting and malnutrition associated with chronic illness</gloss></term>, **hypochondriacal melancholy** <note>original: "melancolia hippocondriaca," a condition believed to originate in the organs of the upper abdomen, causing both digestive distress and low spirits</note>, **jaundice** <note>original: "iteritia," a yellowing of the skin often linked to liver dysfunction</note>, and **dropsy** <note>original: "hidoprifia," the swelling of the body due to excess fluid</note>. It also strengthens the viscera—

<summary>This page describes the preparation of the "Pantagogos" Antidote, a potent alchemical oil made from metallic regulus and spirits of salt, intended to purge the body of various chronic ailments. It is presented as a superior alternative to traditional herbal decoctions for purifying the blood and restoring the appetite.</summary>

# The Lysipyretos Antidote
<note>original: "Antidotto Lysipyretos"; a name derived from the Greek *lysis* (dissolving) and *pyretos* (fever)</note>

### Chapter XVII.

Take four ounces of red <term>Flowers of Antimony<gloss>A powder produced by sublimating the mineral antimony; in this era, it was highly valued for its ability to purge the body of "corrupt humors"</gloss></term> and two ounces of Flowers of Sulfur sublimated until they reach a white color. By working with prudence, one will obtain the aforementioned "flowers" in a deep red state. These shall then be <term>essentialized<gloss>Converted into an "essence" or potent liquid form</gloss></term> first with the <term>Spirit of Vitriol of Venus<gloss>An acidic spirit distilled from copper sulfate</gloss></term>, and afterwards with the true <term>Ethereal Spirit of Saturn<gloss>A highly volatile alchemical solvent derived from lead, likely an early form of acetone; such spirits were often kept secret by masters of the art</gloss></term>. 

By operating as one ought, one will have a true universal <term>febrifuge<gloss>A medicine used to reduce fever</gloss></term>, even for fevers that are malignant and pestilential, by giving four or five drops in an appropriate liquid. However, since the <term>adept<gloss>A practitioner who has mastered the secrets of alchemy</gloss></term> does not know what this "Ethereal Spirit of Saturn" is, he will not be able to reach his goal, and will not have as much of...

### Chapter XXII.

At that point, the spirit shall be extracted until it reaches the consistency of an oil.

One should give three to six drops (in whatever liquid is appropriate for the illness and the patient's **constitution** <note>original: "compleffione"; referring to the individual's unique balance of humors which governed their health</note>)...

# On Antimony according to Millius’s *Reformed Medical and Chemical Dispensatory*, Book 1, Chapter 12; in the *Chemical Basilica*, Book 5, Chapter 1; and its origin, qualities, and virtues according to the ancients and moderns;

### Chapter XXV.

In the *Reformed Medical-Chemical Dispensatory* <note>original: "Antidotario Medico Chimico riformato"</note> on page 28, as a true disciple of the most learned Quercetanus <note>Joseph Duchesne (c. 1544–1609), a French physician who championed the use of chemical remedies over traditional herbal ones</note> and an adopted son of the Hermetic School <note>A philosophical and medical tradition following the teachings of Hermes Trismegistus, focusing on the spiritual and chemical transformation of matter</note>, the author speaks thus in favor of Antimony: "Today, by many who are—I shall add—envious of the well-being of others, Antimony is proclaimed a lethal poison. But according to Dioscorides <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and author of the influential herbal *De Materia Medica*</note> and the ancients, it possesses restrictive <note>astringent</note> and cooling properties, for which reason it is used in eye-washes <term>collyria: liquid medicinal preparations for the eyes</term>. Galen <note>The Roman-era physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for centuries</note> says the same—"

<summary>This page introduces a defense of Antimony as a medical treatment, citing both modern chemical physicians like Millius and Quercetanus and ancient authorities like Dioscorides and Galen to counter claims that it is merely a poison.</summary>
<keywords>Antimony, Millius, Quercetanus, Hermetic School, Dioscorides, Galen, medical chemistry, collyria, pharmacology</keywords>

--- Page 198 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of Antimony's medicinal use. The text begins mid-word, completing the term "medesima" (the same) from the previous page's discussion of Galen and Dioscorides.</meta>

...the same power of drying and astringency is attributed to it in such a way that neither by Dioscorides, nor by the same Galen, nor even by the other ancients is it found numbered among the simple poisons. But let us suppose the case that it were a poison; should its use in medicine be forbidden for that reason? Are not Lapis Lazuli <note>A deep-blue stone used as a pigment and historically as a potent purgative</note> and Armenian Stone <note>original: "Lapis Armeno"; a blue mineral similar to azurite, used in antiquity to treat melancholia</note> both numbered among poisons? And yet their use is not excluded from among purging remedies. Many are displeased by that force and violence in Antimony to purge both by vomiting and by stool <term>secesso: the evacuation of waste from the bowels</term>, which I myself do not praise. Nevertheless, such a vehement and harmful quality is also found in Spurge <term>Titimali: plants of the genus Euphorbia, known for their caustic and purgative milky sap</term>, in Thapsia <note>A genus of plants sometimes called "deadly carrots," used as a powerful skin irritant and purgative</note>, in White Hellebore, and in other kinds of most violent remedies that excite convulsions and belong to the class of poisons—under which, as has been said, Antimony is not included.

# The Calcination of Antimony according to Paracelsus, as described by Mylius in the *Basilica Chymica*, Book 5, Chapter 1, Page 499.

### Chapter XXVI.

Take four pounds of selected <term>Antimony: A metallic substance used in early modern medicine; it was prized for its ability to "purge" the body, though it was poisonous if not processed correctly.</term> and 5 pounds of prepared common salt. Finely powder everything and mix them together. Place the mixture in a wide earthenware pan over a lit charcoal fire, stirring constantly and continuously with an iron spatula until the smoke ceases and the Antimony remains a yellowish color. This will occur within six or eight hours. Take care to watch that it does not melt from the intensity of the fire, and avoid the <note>original: "fumo nociuo"</note> noxious smoke. Once completed, finely powder the entire mass again and infuse it in hot water, washing it several times until every bit of saltiness is removed. Afterward, dry it and <term>sublimate: A process where a solid is heated into a vapor and then cooled back into a solid form to purify it.</term> it without any further addition, applying a graduated fire, and

Having collected the <term>Flowers: In alchemy, this refers to the fine, powdery substance produced through sublimation—heating a solid until it becomes a vapor and then cooling it back into a solid.</term> for other use, take the <term>Caput Mortuum: Literally "dead head," this refers to the residue or remains left in a vessel after a chemical process like distillation or sublimation.</term> and pulverize it. Place it in a vessel to be <term>reverberated: A technique where the heat of a furnace is reflected or deflected back onto the substance being heated to maintain high, even temperatures.</term> in such a way that the fire is graduated <note>Graduated fire refers to a fire whose intensity is slowly and carefully increased over time.</note> and the vessel is sealed so that no flying ash may enter it. However, the said vessel should have some small holes around it so that the fire can perform its action. This process will take place over the course of one month. 

Once this <term>Calcination: The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to oxidize it or change its physical state.</term> is finished, take the said *Caput Mortuum*, which will now be red in color. Once pulverized, place it into a proper vessel with distilled <term>Radical Vinegar: An extremely concentrated and powerful form of vinegar (acetic acid) used by alchemists as a potent solvent.</term>, and the tincture shall be extracted. After the tincture is filtered and the <term>Menstruum: A solvent used to extract the active medicinal "essence" from a substance.</term> has been distilled off, wash the residue several times with some <term>Cordial Water: A medicinal spirit, often distilled with herbs, used to strengthen the heart and vitality.</term> to remove any sharpness of the vinegar, keeping it for use once it is dry. 

One may proceed further by extracting the tincture once more with <term>Spirit of Wine: High-quality, distilled alcohol (ethanol) used for extractions.</term> as has been described in other places. Similarly, one can "fix" <note>To "fix" a substance means to stabilize a volatile material so that it no longer evaporates or changes state when heated.</note> the aforementioned "flowers" and extract a tincture from them, as previously mentioned in the works of <note>Basil Valentine: A legendary and influential alchemist, famously associated with the study of antimony in "The Triumphal Chariot of Antimony."</note> Basil and <note>Quercetanus: The Latinized name of Joseph Duchesne (c. 1544–1609), a prominent French physician who championed the use of chemical remedies over traditional herbal ones.</note> Quercetanus; one will also find the dosage and medicinal virtues of this tincture in their writings.

# Liver of Antimony.

### Chapter XXVII.

Take the best quality antimony and <term>saltpeter: Potassium nitrate, used here as an oxidizing agent to help the antimony burn and transform.</term> in <term>equal parts: Indicated in the original by the term 'ana'.</term>. Pulverize everything and, once mixed, place it in a cup or a small iron pan. Then, ignite it with a red-hot spatula or with a burning coal; it will <term>calcine: The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to change its chemical or physical state, often reducing it to a powder.</term> and remain in a form the color of liver, from which it takes its name <note>original: "fegato"</note>.

The dose of this is twelve to twenty <term>grains: A historical unit of weight based on the weight of a single grain of cereal; approximately 65 milligrams.</term> infused in <term>Blessed Thistle water: A medicinal distillation of *Cnicus benedictus*, highly valued in the Renaissance for treating the plague and high fevers.</term>, or another suitable liquor, for fevers, especially those that are pestilential <note>Likely referring to the plague or highly infectious epidemic diseases.</note>.

From the said liver, pulverized and boiled, is made the true <term>Crocus of Metals: A reddish-yellow powder (antimony oxysulfide) named for its resemblance to the color of saffron, or 'crocus'.</term>, so called because of its saffron color, and because antimony is called the "Root of Metals" and the <term>Magnesia of the Philosophers: A cryptic alchemical term for a primary material that can transform other substances.</term>. The method is found in the third book of the first part of the *Guide to Chemistry*.

# Glass of Antimony without addition.

### Chapter XXVIII.

Take some <term>Antimony: A brittle, silvery semimetal (stibnite) that was a cornerstone of 17th-century "chemical" medicine, used to create potent remedies.</term>, and pulverize it finely. Place it in an earthenware <term>pan: The original 'tegamo' refers to a shallow vessel; here it must be unglazed to prevent the antimony from reacting with or sticking to a ceramic glaze.</term> that resists fire and is not glazed. Spread the antimony across the bottom of the pan so that it is no thicker than the <note>original: "coſta di cortello"</note> back of a knife. Place it over burning coals to <term>calcine: To heat a substance to high temperatures in open air to remove impurities or change its chemical state through oxidation.</term>, regulating the fire so that the antimony does not clump together. Should clumping occur, return it to be crushed and calcined again as before, stirring constantly with a small iron rod or something similar.

Continue this calcination until the antimony is reduced to a white or gray ash, and until, by placing a small amount of it upon a lit coal or a glowing iron, it no longer releases smoke. Once this is done, place the ash while still hot into a <term>crucible: A heat-resistant container used for melting materials at very high temperatures.</term> over a most violent fire to melt. Pour the resulting glass onto a piece of heated marble or into a copper basin, and you will have a most beautiful and excellent <term>Glass of Antimony: A translucent, orange-red substance (technically antimony oxysulfide) used as a powerful medicine to induce vomiting and purging.</term>.

## Salt of Antimony.

### Chapter XXIX.

Before turning the aforementioned <term>Calx: The powdery residue remaining after a substance (in this case, antimony) has been roasted or "calcined" to remove impurities.</term> into glass, let it boil for five or six continuous hours. When this water has been <term>filtered: Strained through cloth or paper to remove solid particles.</term> and <term>coagulated: In this context, the process of evaporating the liquid to leave behind the solid crystalline salts.</term> according to the Art, you will obtain one <term>drachm: A historical unit of apothecary weight, roughly 3.9 grams or 1/8th of an ounce.</term> of Salt of Antimony for every pound of calx. This salt is not inferior in its medicinal virtues to the Salt of Gold <note>original: "Sale del oro"; likely referring to a highly prized alchemical preparation of gold believed to have universal healing properties.</note>. The remaining calx, after it has been dried, can still be reduced into glass as described in the previous chapter. The dosage is from two to four grains in a suitable liquid.

It cleanses the blood of every impurity and heals the <term>French Disease: A common historical name for syphilis.</term>. It is excellent for <term>gout: A painful form of inflammatory arthritis, often called "podagra" in early modern texts.</term> and breaks internal <term>abscesses: Referred to in the text as "apostemes," these are localized collections of pus caused by infection.</term>; therefore, it is excellent for <term>pleurisy: Inflammation of the linings of the lungs and chest cavity, causing sharp pain.</term>, and it drives away fevers. When applied externally, it cleanses malignant ulcers.

# Glass of Antimony with Gold.

### Chapter XXX.

Then, pulverize it and <term>calcine: To roast a substance at high heat to remove volatile components or change its chemical state.</term> it as was said above regarding Antimony. It shall then be cast into glass. 

If, after this <term>vitrification: The process of turning a substance into a glass-like solid through intense heat.</term>, some regulus remains, you can mix it again with a little more regulus of Antimony, calcine it as before, and vitrify it. Alternatively, one could, according to the Art, use the breath of the <term>bellows: A device for blowing air into a fire; here used to oxidize and evaporate the antimony, a process similar to cupellation.</term> to drive away the Antimony that is in the said regulus and recover what little gold remains there. 

Now, place <term>Spirit of Wine: High-proof distilled alcohol, similar to modern ethanol.</term> over this crushed glass and leave it in <term>digestion: The process of soaking a substance in a liquid under gentle, consistent heat to extract its medicinal properties.</term> until the liquid is colored. # Tincture of the Sulfur of
# Antimony.

### Chapter XXXII.

Make a <term>lye: A caustic alkaline solution used in chemical extractions.</term> with <term>calcined tartar: Potassium carbonate produced by heating the crusty deposits from wine barrels.</term> and wood ashes <note>original: "ceneri di fuggire," likely referring to potash-rich wood ashes or beechwood ashes.</note>, or else take the lye used by soap-makers. In that liquid, boil the finely pulverized antimony until the said lye has become red as blood. Then, strain it and pour vinegar over it, and a powder will precipitate to the bottom. Dry this powder, and afterward <term>sublimate: To heat a substance into a vapor so it collects as a purified solid.</term> it with an equal part of <term>sal ammoniac: Ammonium chloride, a salt used to help minerals vaporize or dissolve.</term>. ...distilled vinegar, digesting it for eight days. Then, let it be distilled until red drops begin to appear; at that moment, change the receiver. Once the distillation is finished, take the <term>Antimony: A brittle, silvery-gray metalloid that was a staple of "iatrochemistry" (chemistry-based medicine); it was used to purge the body of "bad humors" by inducing vomiting or sweating.</term> remaining at the bottom of the <term>retort: A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling or decomposing substances through heat.</term> or flask and reduce it to powder. For every pound of this powder, add six ounces of finely powdered sugar candy. Placing it all into a retort and attaching the receiver which already contains the previously distilled oil, let it be distilled with graduated fire, increasing the heat at the end until nothing more is seen to come out. Once this is done, separate the <term>Spirit of Wine: Highly concentrated alcohol (ethanol), often used as a solvent or preservative in early modern medicine.</term> using a <term>water bath: Also known as a "bain-marie," this technique involves placing a vessel inside another filled with hot water to provide gentle, even heat.</term>, and the oil shall be preserved in a sealed glass vessel.

It is a most useful medicine for many infirmities, particularly for <note>Dropsy (original: "hidropisia"): A historical medical term for the swelling of soft tissues due to the accumulation of excess water, known today as edema.</note>, as it gently and without distress induces vomiting and moves the body <note>i.e., acts as a laxative to purge the system.</note>.

The Dose is from three drops up to six or eight at most, taken in wine, broth, or another suitable liquid. It can also be mixed with some preserve according to the patient’s preference. It should also be noted that many times it only moves the Body <note>Meaning it may act only as a laxative rather than an emetic (vomit-inducing) agent.</note>.

distilled vinegar, Antimony, sugar candy, oil, Spirit of Wine, dropsy, Dose
