# Oil of Flowers of Antimony by the aforementioned method

### Chapter 45.

Take <term>aqua fortis: A highly corrosive mineral acid, usually nitric acid; its name literally means "strong water."</term> made from <term>Vitriol: A historical name for sulfate minerals, often used to create powerful acids.</term> and Salt, three pounds of each, and six pounds of <term>bole: A type of medicinal clay (often "Bole Armoniac") used in distillation to prevent the glass from breaking or to regulate heat.</term> or clay. Let this be very well <term>dephlegmated: The process of removing "phlegm" (excess water) from a substance, typically through evaporation or distillation, to concentrate the active spirits.</term>. Then take the white <term>flowers of Antimony: The powdery sublimate produced when antimony is heated; so called because they "bloom" on the neck of the heating vessel.</term> and place them in a flask. Pour enough of the aforementioned aqua fortis over them so that it floats above the powder by two or three fingers' depth. Leave them thus for six or eight days in a warm place.

Afterward, distill the said water until it reaches dryness; then, pour more fresh water over it, repeating this process many times until the water comes out as acidic as it was when put in. This is the sign that the "flowers" are sufficiently impregnated with the <term>Spirit: The volatile, active essence of a substance, believed to carry its medicinal or chemical power.</term>. At that point, they shall be taken and placed in a <term>retort: A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling or decomposing substances through heat.</term> to be distilled. First, separate a watery moisture; once that has come out, change the receiver, and a white, heavy oil will be collected, similar to <term>butter of Antimony: Antimony trichloride; it was called "butter" due to its thick, oily, or buttery consistency at room temperature.</term>, leaving at the bottom a small amount of useless <term>caput mortuum: A Latin term meaning "dead head," used by alchemists to refer to the worthless residue or dregs left over after a chemical process like distillation.</term>.

The said Oil is the same as the butter of Antimony, and it has the same—

When precipitated into common water by distilling or <term>rectifying: The process of purifying a liquid by repeated distillation to increase its potency.</term> it—and once the water is placed in the receiver—a white powder will precipitate. This will create a purgative similar to the powder called <term>Emetic: A substance that induces vomiting.</term> or <term>Algaroth: Named after the physician Vittorio Algarotti, this is antimony oxychloride, a powerful medicine used in the 17th century to purge the body.</term> (see my *Guide to Chemistry*, Part One, Book 3). This purge acts through both vomiting and bowel movements.

It can also be prepared in a form that purges only from below <note>meaning it acts as a laxative rather than an emetic.</note>. Take equal parts of the aforementioned Oil and <term>Oil of Tartar: A concentrated solution of potassium carbonate, produced when "salt of tartar" is allowed to melt or "deliquesce" by absorbing moisture from the air.</term>. Leave them combined for two days in a warm place; then it will precipitate, and it should be <term>sweetened: In alchemy, "sweetening" (edulcoration) means washing a precipitate with water to remove any lingering corrosive acids or salts.</term> by washing it at the end with some <term>cordial water: A medicinal liquid, often herbal, intended to invigorate the heart and vital spirits.</term>.

The dose for both the one and the other is from one to four grains.

## Saccharine Oil of Antimony by Béguin.
<note>original: "Beguino." Referring to Jean Béguin (c. 1550–1620), a French apothecary whose work "Tirocinium Chymicum" was one of the first influential textbooks on chemistry.</note>

### Chapter 47.

Béguin describes many operations involving <term>Antimony: A metallic mineral used in early chemistry to create powerful emetics (substances that cause vomiting) and topical balms.</term> in his *Apprenticeship* <note>original: "Tirocinio," referring to Béguin's book "Tirocinium Chymicum" (1610).</note>, but as they are similar to many others presented here by various authors, I shall not bother to include anything else but the aforementioned oil, along with the method of making from it certain <term>febrifuge: A medicine intended to reduce or dispel a fever.</term> pills; now, the oil is made as follows. Take four ounces each of Antimony and <term>Sugar Candy: Zuccaro Candido; highly refined white sugar crystallized into hard, clear chunks.</term>, and one ounce of <term>burnt alum: Alume bruciato; alum that has been heated until it becomes porous and white, used for its drying and caustic properties.</term>. Let it all be ground together, then place it in a large <term>retort: A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids.</term> to <term>distill: The process of heating a substance to capture its essence or volatile components as a liquid.</term> according to the art. A thick oil will be extracted, which is a most perfect remedy for ulcers and malignant or <term>cancerous: Cancrose; a term used historically for persistent, spreading, or corrosive sores and tumors.</term> wounds, applied over them with ointments or appropriate oils, or by anointing the affliction itself.

Take one pound of the said **Oil**, four drams of **Socotrine Aloe** <note>original: "Aloe Socotrino." A resin from the Socotra aloe plant, prized in pharmacy for its potent laxative properties.</note>, two drams of **Ambergris** <note>original: "Ambra griſa." A rare, waxy substance produced by sperm whales, used both in perfumery and as a fixative in medicines.</note>, and 2 and a half drams of Oriental **Saffron**. Form these into pills according to the Art; the dose is from twelve to twenty grains.

Or it is better in this way: a tincture shall be extracted from the said **Oil** with <term>divine spirit: Spirito divino; likely referring to highly rectified spirit of wine (alcohol) used as a powerful alchemical solvent.</term> according to the art. After the Spirit has been separated by a <term>bath: Bagno; a "bain-marie" or water bath, used to provide gentle, indirect heat.</term>, there will remain at the bottom a red tincture, like blood. One shall take the aforementioned dose of this and, with the other ingredients, make pills as described above; however, in place of the Saffron, one shall add one dram of the essence. And thus we shall bring the second book to a close.

# Peter John Faber in his *Chemical Secrets*, book 3, Chapter 9, speaking of Antimony.

### Chapter III.

In the aforementioned place, Faber, speaking of the generation and production of **Antimony**, and its other specific qualities, says the following at the end: "Many among the **Galenic Physicians** <note>Followers of the ancient Greek physician Galen, who generally preferred plant-based medicines and were often suspicious of the "new" chemical or mineral-based medicines of the Renaissance.</note> consider **Antimony** to be a pure **Poison**, and for this reason, they banish it from their pharmacopeias <note>original: "Antidotari." Collections of recipes for medicines and antidotes.</note>, not permitting it to be accepted as any remedy for the treatment of diseases. They say it is a **"Domestic Lion"** <note>A metaphor for a powerful force that is helpful when tamed but capable of turning on its owner if handled incorrectly.</note> that many times kills and devours its own master.

But if these good men would labor and toil in search of the infinite **Virtues** and properties in which **Antimony** abounds, they would take up a *recantation* <note>original: "Palinodia." A poem or song in which the author retracts a view expressed in a former poem.</note> to sweetly sing a thousand praises and hymns to the **Creator** who created it, etc. **Gout** <note>term: gout. A form of inflammatory arthritis characterized by recurrent attacks of a red, tender, hot, and swollen joint.</note> cannot be healed with anything other than **Antimony**, which also heals **Stones** <note>original: "Calcol." Refers to kidney or bladder stones.</note> and cures **leprosy** <note>term: leprosy. In this period, "leprosy" often referred to various severe skin diseases as well as Hansen's disease.</note>; and among

The other preparations of **Antimony** that he describes in other places in his *Spagyric Surgery* <note>Spagyric medicine refers to the production of herbal and mineral medicines using alchemical procedures like fermentation, distillation, and extraction.</note> are taught in the second Section, chapter 9, where he provides a most beautiful recipe for making a **Balsam of Antimony** as follows.

## Miraculous Balsam of Antimony by Faber.

### Chapter IV.

Take raw **Antimony** (that is, straight from the mine) and **sublimated Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurio solimato." Also known as corrosive sublimate or mercuric chloride.</note>, two pounds of each <note>original: "anna." A pharmaceutical term meaning "of each the same amount."</note>; grind them into a fine powder and mix them. Placing the mixture in a **retort** <note>term: retort. A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distillation.</note>, distill it over a slow fire. A gum, or rather a **butter** <note>term: butter of antimony. Antimony trichloride, which has a thick, oily, or buttery consistency at room temperature.</note>, will form; as this obstructs the neck of the retort, it should be made to fall into the receiver by bringing the fire closer to the neck of the retort. 

Over the said rectified substance, pour pure spring water, or even better, distilled rainwater; it will become white like milk. After it has settled, pour off the water by tilting the vessel and add fresh water, repeating this many times until the powder is **sweetened** <note>original: "dolcificata." In alchemy, this refers to the process of washing a substance (edulcoration) until all corrosive salts are removed and it no longer tastes acrid.</note>. Finally, the powder can be washed with some **cordial water** <note>A medicinal liquid intended to stimulate the heart or improve the spirit.</note> and then left to dry.

<meta>This page continues the specific laboratory instructions for preparing the Balsam of Antimony, following the washing (edulcoration) of the powder described on the previous page. It details the extraction of the medicinal essence using vinegar and then rectified spirit.</meta>

...preserving it for use, as can be seen in the *Guide to Chemistry* <note>original: *Guida alla Chimica*. This refers to a chemical manual, likely the one by Annibale Barlet or a similar contemporary work.</note> printed in **Modena**. The water, which will have become acetous <note>original: *acetosa*. Meaning having the qualities of vinegar or becoming acidic.</note>, should be placed in a **flask** <note>original: *bozza*. A glass vessel used for distillation.</note> to be distilled until it begins to come out acidic; at that point, the distillation shall be stopped. Take what remains at the bottom of the flask and place it in a **sample jar** <note>original: *saggiolo*. A small glass vessel used for testing or small-batch reactions.</note> over the aforementioned **Antimony**, which must be very finely powdered. 

Let it digest over an **ash fire** <note>term: ash fire. A gentle heat source created by burying the vessel in hot ashes to maintain a steady, low temperature.</note> for one month, taking care that the neck or mouth of the jar is very well sealed so that it does not "breathe" [leak vapor]. Once the liquid is well-colored—that is, a **ruby-colored vinegar**—pour it off by tilting the vessel, and add fresh liquid, digesting it as before. Repeat this until the vinegar no longer takes on any color. 

When all the **tinctures** <note>term: tincture. A medicinal extract where the active properties of a substance are dissolved in a solvent like vinegar or alcohol.</note> have been combined, they shall be distilled over the gentle heat of a water bath until the drops are seen to turn yellowish. At that point, remove it and pulverize the residue remaining at the bottom of the flask. Pour over this residue some **rectified Divine Spirit** <note>original: *Spirito divino rettificato*. In this context, it refers to highly purified and distilled alcohol.</note> so that it floats three or four fingers' breadth above the powder. Having sealed the mouth very well, let it digest until the **Spirit of Wine** is deeply colored. This shall then be poured off by tilting the vessel, proceeding in this manner until the entire tincture has been extracted; likewise—

...with **Spirit of wine** <note>original: *Spirito di vino*. Highly distilled alcohol or ethanol, used as a solvent in alchemy.</note>, and afterwards, by the gentle **heat of a water bath** <note>term: water bath. A method of heating a vessel by placing it in hot water (a *bain-marie*) to ensure a steady, low temperature that does not exceed the boiling point of water.</note>, the aforementioned tinted Spirit shall be distilled until it begins to distill yellowish drops. At that point, the receiver <note>original: *recipiente*. The glass vessel positioned to collect the condensed vapors during distillation.</note> is changed, and one receives the true, marvelous **Balsam of Antimony**. It is effective not only for healing any long-standing <note>original: *inveterata*. Referring to chronic or old wounds that have failed to heal.</note> wound, but it is also a nearly universal remedy for curing countless well-known diseases.

The dose is from two to four drops in an appropriate liquid.

# **Saffron of Metals** <note>original: *Croco de Metalli*. Also known as Crocus Metallorum, this is a liver-colored substance (usually an oxysulfide) produced by the rapid oxidation of antimony.</note> of **Martin Ruland** <note>original: *Martino Rolando*. A renowned 16th-century German physician and alchemist known for his "Golden Rule" and chemical medicines.</note> with other Operations of **Antimony** published by **Ehrenfried Hagendorn** <note>original: *Eherenfredo Hagendorni*. A 17th-century German physician and author of several medical and botanical treatises.</note>, Doctor of Medicine.

...for a short time to **calcine** <term>calcine. To heat a substance to a high temperature in order to cause chemical change or oxidation, usually reducing it to a powder.</term>, then it should be removed and set to cool. Afterwards, let it be pulverized and washed very well with fresh spring water, just once, to remove the remaining **nitrous saltiness** <note>original: *salsedine nitrosa*. The leftover salty residue from the nitre (saltpeter) used in the chemical reaction.</note>; afterwards, if necessary, it is stored for use.

This **Saffron** <note>original: *Croco*. Referring here to the Saffron of Metals, a chemical preparation of antimony named for its deep orange-red color.</note> is the base of the **Blessed Water of Ruland** <note>original: *acqua benedetta del Rulando*. A famous emetic (vomit-inducing) medicine in the 17th century, made by infusing Crocus Metallorum in wine.</note>, as will be stated further on. The aforementioned author, in his *Eighth Century*, Cure 29 <note>term: Century. A common medical book format of the era, where a physician published one hundred successful clinical cases or "cures."</note>, prescribes it for **Cardialgia** <note>original: *Cardialgia*. In historical medicine, this referred to severe stomach pain or "heartburn" rather than a heart condition.</note> in the quantity of a pea-sized amount macerated in three or four ounces of water and wine, boiled briefly, and then administered. For **pleurisy** <note>original: *pleuresia*. An inflammation of the linings around the lungs, causing painful breathing.</note>, in the *Seventh Century*, Cure 67, he gives wine in which the **Saffron of Metals** has been infused. In the *Fourth Century*, Cure 10, he applies it externally to remove **unhealthy and dead flesh** <note>original: *Carne cattiva, e morta*. Necrotic or infected tissue that prevents a wound from healing.</note> that forms in sores, by sprinkling it on top. In the *Sixth Century*, Cure 71, he applies it with fennel water and **Oil of Vitriol** <note>term: Oil of Vitriol. Concentrated sulfuric acid, used here in a diluted or compounded form for its caustic properties.</note> for painful **growths** <note>original: *condoglomati*. Likely a corruption of "condylomata," referring to wart-like growths or calluses.</note>—that is, the formation of callous flesh on the fingers and toes, and also for growths on the backside.

# Blessed Water of the same.

### Chapter VI.

Take one ounce of the aforementioned **Saffron of Metals** <term>Saffron of Metals. Also known as *Crocus Metallorum*, this was an orange-red chemical preparation made from antimony, used as a powerful emetic to induce vomiting.</term> in powder form, and two or three pounds of wine, or some distilled water—such as **Blessed Thistle** <note>original: *Cardo Santo*. A plant commonly used in the Renaissance to treat the plague and digestive ailments.</note>, **Speedwell** <note>original: *Veronica*.</note>, **Wild Thyme** <note>original: *Serpillo*.</note>, **Mint**, or other similar herbs—or rainwater or spring water. Once mixed, let it **digest** <term>digest. In early chemistry, this meant to steep a substance in a liquid with gentle, consistent heat to extract its medicinal properties.</term> in a closed glass vessel over low heat for two or three days; afterward, let it be strained and kept for use.

The same **Ruland** <note>Martin Ruland the Elder (1532–1602), a German physician who championed the use of chemical and antimonial remedies.</note>, in his fifth **Century** <term>Century. A popular medical book format consisting of 100 successful clinical case studies or "cures."</term>, cure 95, affirms that generally the aforementioned water, given by itself or mixed with something else, has been applied to every infirmity—even those deemed incurable—resulting in the health of the patients. Particularly in the fourth century, cure 15, he gave one and a half ounces of this with one and a half ounces of olive oil; and in the second century, cure 22, in water for **Melancholy** <note>original: *Melancolia*. Under the ancient system of "humors," this was a physical and mental condition caused by an excess of black bile.</note>; and in the fourth century, cure 70, in wine for **hypochondriac passion** <note>original: *passione hippocondriaca*. A historical diagnosis for distress felt in the upper abdomen, often linked to the spleen or liver and associated with anxiety.</note>. In the third century, number 91, he gives of it up to

## Crocus of Metals, which Tentzel describes in the Exegesis, called Genuine by Ruland.

->Chapter XI.<-

Take choice **Antimony** and **Saltpeter** <note>original: "Salnitro," potassium nitrate, used as an oxidizing agent in this chemical reaction.</note> in equal parts; let them be finely powdered and, once mixed, put them into a **crucible** or a pot with a **luted** <term>Luted. Sealed with a paste (lute) of clay and other materials to make the container airtight and heat-resistant during high-temperature operations.</term> lid on top, having a hole in the middle through which the **Arsenical spirits** <note>original: "spiriti Arsenicali," the toxic and pungent fumes released when heating raw antimony ore, which frequently contained arsenic impurities.</note> can exhale. Then place the said crucible into a melting fire with bellows, blowing until everything is fused and until the smoke ceases; at that point, the fire shall be increased for a quarter of an hour. After this, remove it from the fire and let it cool; it should then be broken, and the salts shall be separated from the **Crocus** <term>Crocus of Metals. Also known as Crocus Metallorum. A liver-colored or reddish-orange powder (antimony oxysulfide) used as a potent emetic and purgative in early modern medicine.</term>, which, when finely powdered, will be as red as **Cinnabar** <note>original: "Cinabro," a bright red mineral (mercury sulfide). The author uses this comparison to indicate the high quality and proper chemical transformation of the powder.</note>; and the way to use it in medicine is as follows:

A German physician whose pharmacopoeia became the standard reference for 17th-century pharmacy.</term>, having described them clearly so that everyone may operate with ease.

A potassium salt (potassium bitartrate) found in wine barrels, used as a flux in chemical reactions.</term> *ana* <note>original: "anna," a common pharmaceutical abbreviation from the Greek *ana*, meaning "of each" or "in equal parts."</note>; let them be **cemented** <term>Cementation. A process of heating a solid in a powder to effect a chemical change without melting the substance into a mass.</term> for four or five hours over a strong fire. Then, throw it while still hot into a quantity of **vinegar**, and let it boil for two hours; after this, strain it, and it coagulates into a powder. Then take some of this powder—which is the **Sulfur of Antimony** <term>Sulfur of Antimony. Not common sulfur, but the "soul" or combustible, active principle of the antimony mineral.</term>—one part, and **burnt vitriol** <term>Burnt Vitriol. Iron or copper sulfate that has been heated (calcined) until it loses its water content and turns into a dry, concentrated powder.</term> two parts, and mix...

# Tincture, or Oil of Antimony; a Febrifuge and Antipleuretic by the same [Author].

->Chapter XVI.<-

Take <term>Regulus of Antimony</term> <note>The metallic form of antimony, purified from its ore.</note> calcined with a <term>burning mirror</term> <note>A parabolic mirror or lens used to concentrate sunlight to create the intense heat required for chemical or alchemical transformation.</note> as much as you like; pour over it <term>Spirit of Vitriol</term> <note>An early term for sulfuric acid.</note> so that it rises above it by one finger’s width, and let it digest in a warm place for two days. Then, remove the liquid by decanting <note>original: "per inclinatione," literally "by tilting" the vessel to pour off the liquid without disturbing the sediment.</note>, and replace it with more [spirit], digesting as before, and repeating this until it is seen that the said Spirit dissolves the said Antimony. Then, using a sand bath <note>original: "per cenere," literally "by ash," a method of providing gentle, indirect heat by burying the glass vessel in hot ashes or sand.</note>, the <term>phlegm</term> <note>The watery, less potent part of a distilled substance.</note> shall be separated—all the aforementioned Spirit having been combined—and an Oil will remain. Over this Oil, one shall distill (having first allowed it to digest) <term>Spirit of Wine</term> <note>High-proof alcohol or ethanol.</note>, and it will be finished.

The dose is four drops.

# Magistery of Antimony by Kesler, described by the aforementioned [Author].

### Chapter XVII.

Take half a pound of Antimony, four ounces of <term>burnt alum</term> <note>Potassium aluminum sulfate that has been heated to remove its water content, making it more reactive.</note>, and half a pound each of Salt and <term>Bole</term> <note>original: "bolo anna," referring to a medicinal earth or clay, likely "Bole Armoniac." The term "anna" or "ana" is a pharmaceutical shorthand meaning "of each."</note>. Crush each ingredient separately, mix them together, and place them to distill in a <term>retort</term> <note>A glass vessel with a long, curved neck used for distilling liquids or spirits.</note>. First, the <term>phlegm</term> <note>The watery, non-medicinal part of the distillation.</note> will come out (which shall be separated and set aside); afterward, a white Spirit will emerge, and then a reddish one. If this should stick to the neck of the vessel in the form of ice, bring a hot coal close to it with tongs so that the substance may be dissolved. 

Then, it shall be <term>rectified</term> <note>Refined and purified by being distilled again.</note>, and in that spirit, gold leaf shall be dissolved. In this solution, one shall add <term>Spirit of Wine</term> <note>High-proof distilled alcohol.</note>, <term>circulating</term> <note>A process where the liquid is heated so that it evaporates and condenses back into itself repeatedly in a closed container.</note> it for six weeks. Afterward, the spirit shall be separated from the <term>dregs</term> <note>original: "feci," the solid sediment or waste remaining at the bottom.</note> that will have formed at the bottom. By pouring distilled water over the aforementioned spirit, a white powder will precipitate to the bottom; this is the **Magistery of Antimony**.

It purges gently through vomiting and through the bowels; and it has all the virtues... <note>The text ends here due to the page break; the catchword "to" suggests the next page begins with "totally" or "total."</note>

<column-break/>

<term>Magistery of Antimony</term> <note>A "magistery" is an alchemical term for a preparation where the essence of a substance is extracted and then reconstituted into a new form, usually a potent powder.</note>, <term>Kesler</term> <note>Refers to Thomas Kessler, a 17th-century chemist and author of "Laboratorium Chymicum."</note>, **burnt alum**, **Bole Armoniac**, **retort**, **phlegm**, **White Spirit**, **Spirit of Wine**, **distilled water**.

## Tincture of Antimony by the aforementioned [Author].

### Chapter XVIII.

One fixes the refined <term>saltpeter</term> <note>Potassium nitrate, a key chemical in early pharmacy and alchemy.</note> with sulfur, which is recognized as finished when the said saltpeter remains porous, that is, full of holes. Then, take equal parts of the said saltpeter and Antimony and place them in a <term>reverberatory furnace</term> <note>A furnace that reflects heat from its roof onto the material being heated, allowing for very high temperatures without the fuel touching the substance.</note> for five continuous days, managing the fire in such a way that the <term>crucible</term> <note>A ceramic or graphite container used for melting substances at high heat.</note> is always glowing red-hot. Once this is done, wash the said Antimony several times to remove the nitrous saltiness from it. 

Then, once dried, the tincture shall be extracted with <term>distilled vinegar</term> <note>Used here as a solvent to pull the medicinal properties out of the mineral.</note> according to the art. This tincture shall be <term>exalted</term> <note>Improved or concentrated.</note> with <term>Spirit of Wine</term> <note>High-proof distilled alcohol.</note> after having removed the aforementioned vinegar, as has already been taught in several places.

This tincture purges through evacuation (but more so through sweating); the dosage is from 2 grains up to 4. Furthermore, the <term>earth</term> <note>original: "terra." The solid residue or "dregs" remaining after the liquid extraction.</note> remaining after the extraction of the vinegar is excellent.

# Kesler’s Compound Vinegar for many Operations of Antimony.

### Chapter XIX.

Take dried <term>Vitriol</term> <note>A sulfate of iron or copper, commonly used as a starting material for strong acids.</note> and <term>Quicklime</term> <note>Calcium oxide, a caustic chemical produced by heating limestone.</note>, one pound of each; <term>Sal Ammoniac</term> <note>Ammonium chloride, used here to increase the reactivity of the solvent.</note>, four ounces; and <term>distilled vinegar</term>, three pounds. Place all these into a <term>luted retort</term> <note>A glass distillation vessel sealed with "lute" (a protective paste or clay) to withstand high heat and prevent leaks.</note> and leave it to <term>digest</term> <note>To soak in gentle heat to extract active components.</note> for twenty-four hours. Then, let it be distilled in the same manner as one makes <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>Nitric acid; the text refers to a high-heat distillation method used for strong mineral acids.</note>. After it has been distilled, <term>rectify</term> <note>To purify a substance by redistilling it.</note> it over an equal portion of salt, and preserve it for use.

Since simple distilled vinegar is useless for the extraction of Antimony, whenever you find a requirement for distilled vinegar to perform an extraction, you may use the aforementioned compound vinegar, and you shall not err.

## Clodius’s Ruby of Antimony as described by Schroeder.

### Chapter XX.

Take choice <term>Antimony</term> <note>A brittle, silvery-gray metalloid used in alchemy to create powerful medicines.</note> and <term>tartar</term> <note>Potassium bitartrate, a byproduct of winemaking used here as a flux to help the minerals melt.</note>, <gloss>of each</gloss> <note>The original reads "anna," a common scribal error for "ana," meaning "of each" or "equal parts."</note>; let them be melted together. Take two ounces of the aforementioned mass—or rather the <term>regulus</term> <note>The metallic portion of the antimony that settles at the bottom of the crucible after smelting.</note>—and two ounces of <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>Liquid mercury.</note> that has been optimally purified. Mix everything together and let it <term>digest</term> <note>To soak or heat gently over a long period to encourage chemical change.</note> at a gentle heat until it reaches dryness. Then, a most red <term>tincture</term> <note>A medicinal extract, usually using alcohol as the solvent.</note> shall be extracted with <term>Spirit of Wine</term> <note>Ethanol or highly distilled alcohol.</note> according to the rules of the art. Afterwards, the Spirit shall be drawn off <note>The alcohol is evaporated or distilled away.</note> until it reaches the consistency of honey; thus one will have a material that, upon cooling, will harden into a stone as transparent as a <term>Ruby</term> <note>A "Ruby of Antimony" refers to the deep red, glass-like appearance of the final chemical product, not the gemstone itself.</note>.

It is both purgative and <term>diaphoretic</term> <note>A substance that induces sweating, believed to expel toxins from the body.</note>; the dose is from two to six <term>grains</term> <note>A traditional unit of pharmaceutical weight, approximately 65 milligrams.</note>.

# Schroeder’s Sulfurated Water of Antimony

### Chapter XXI.

Take choice <term>Antimony</term> <note>A metallic mineral often used in early medicine for its powerful transformative effects.</note>, refined <term>Saltpeter</term> <note>Potassium nitrate, used here as an oxidizing agent to help the mixture ignite.</note>, and pure Sulfur, <gloss>equal parts of each</gloss> <note>The original uses "anna," a common scribal error for the Latin "ana," meaning "of equal parts."</note>; let them be pulverized and mixed together. Then, let them be distilled in a large <term>earthenware retort</term> <note>A glass or ceramic flask with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distillation.</note> that has a small tube or pipe on the top through which to add the material little by little. When the retort has become red-hot, the material will immediately ignite upon being added and will pass as smoke into the <term>receiver</term> <note>The vessel that collects the distilled liquid.</note>, which must be of great capacity and contain a little water. This receiver should be well-sealed and <term>luted</term> <note>Sealed with "lute," a chemically resistant paste or clay used to make joints airtight.</note> to the neck of the retort.

One must also take care to immediately plug the aforementioned pipe after having thrown the material into the retort, and not open it until the fumes in the receiver have entirely ceased. At that time, one may proceed to throw in new material. Once the distillation is finished, let it be filtered; or, if one prefers to separate the <term>phlegm</term> <note>The watery, tasteless part of a distillate.</note> and remove the <term>empyreuma</term> <note>A burnt, smoky smell or taste resulting from the use of high heat.</note>, it shall be <term>rectified</term> <note>Re-distilled to increase purity.</note> in a <term>water bath</term> <note>A "Bain-marie," heating the vessel in boiling water rather than over a direct flame to ensure gentle heat.</note>.

It thins and expels the <term>humors</term> <note>The four vital fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) that pre-modern medicine sought to keep in balance.</note>.

<summary>This page describes the preparation of "Sulfurated Water of Antimony," a volatile medicinal spirit produced by igniting a mixture of antimony, sulfur, and saltpeter in a specialized retort. The resulting vapor is captured in water and refined to treat bodily humors.</summary>

# Hartmann’s Panacea of Antimony

->Chapter XXIV.<-

Hartmann <note>Johann Hartmann (1568–1631) was the first professor of "Chymiatry" (chemical medicine) in Europe, known for bridging the gap between traditional medicine and alchemy.</note> has described many operations involving Antimony, but so as not to be long-winded, I will speak briefly.

To make the **Panacea** <note>A legendary universal remedy or "cure-all" sought by alchemists to treat any ailment.</note>, one takes **Cinnabar of Antimony** <note>original: "Cinabro d’ Antimonio." A deep red mineral compound of sulfur and antimony.</note> (the method for making it is found in our *Guide*) and, having powdered it finely, let it boil for three or four hours in a very strong **lye** <note>original: "liscia." An alkaline solution used here as a solvent.</note> made of equal parts of **Calcined Tartar** <note>Potassium carbonate, produced by heating the sediment from wine barrels.</note> and **quicklime** <note>original: "calcina viua." Calcium oxide, a caustic substance used to increase the strength of the lye.</note>. Then, separate the lye from the **running Mercury** <note>Liquid elemental mercury, which separates and settles at the bottom of the vessel.</note> that will be found at the bottom, and filter it. Afterward, let it sit, and the **sulfur** will sink to the bottom; the lye should be separated from this, and then it should be washed with water several times to **sweeten it** <note>In alchemy, "sweetening" or "dulcifying" means washing a substance with water until all traces of caustic or salty chemicals are removed.</note>. Once dried, take one ounce of the said sulfur with another ounce of **Regulus of Antimony** <note>original: "Regolo d’ Antimonio." The pure, metallic form of antimony.</note> and three ounces of **Oil of Sulfur made by the bell** <note>original: "oglio di solfo fatto per Campana." Sulfuric acid created by burning sulfur under a glass bell jar to capture the vapors.</note>, or **Oil of Vitriol** <note>A highly concentrated, corrosive sulfuric acid.</note>. Place everything to **digest** <note>The process of heating a substance gently and continuously to encourage chemical breakdown or fusion.</note> for eight days in a warm place; then, distill it in a **retort** <note>A glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids.</note> in a **sand bath** <note>original: "per arena." A container filled with sand that is heated to provide a gentle, even temperature to the glass vessel nestled inside.</note>, **cohobating** <note>original: "corbãdo," a contraction for *cohobando*. This is the alchemical practice of pouring the distilled liquid back onto the solid remains in the vessel and redistilling it to increase its potency.</note> at least for

## Tincture of Sulfur of Antimony by Hartmann.

### Chapter XXVI.

Make the sulfur of raw antimony by pulverizing it finely and letting it digest for six or seven days in a **lye** <note>original: "liscia."</note> made of five pounds of **Quicklime** <note>original: "Calcina." # Purgative Spirit of Antimonial Vitriol, and Vitriolated Tartar likewise purgative, derived from the same.

### Chapter XXVII.

Take **Glass of Antimony** <note>original: "Vetro d’Antimonio." A transparent, reddish-brown glass-like substance produced by roasting and then melting antimony ore; it was a common starting material in 17th-century pharmacy.</note> in any quantity you please, and let it be reduced to an impalpable powder. Place it in a **small vial** <note>original: "sagiolo." A small glass testing vessel or flask.</note>, pouring over it enough **Spirit of Vitriol** <note>original: "Spirito di Vitriolo." Sulfuric acid, a strong mineral acid used here as a solvent.</note> so that it floats two fingers' breadth above it. Let it be set to **digest** <note>original: "digerire." The chemical process of soaking a solid in a liquid under gentle, steady heat to extract its active components.</note> at a slow heat; a blackish extraction will be produced, which purges the bowels.

The dose is from six to twelve drops, in broth or another liquid. From one part of the aforementioned tinted Spirit and two parts of **Oil of Tartar** <note>original: "oglio di tartaro." A thick, liquid form of potassium carbonate, usually created by letting refined tartar absorb moisture from the air.</note>, let **Vitriolated Tartar** <note>original: "tartaro vitriolato." Potassium sulfate, produced by the reaction of the acid (spirit of vitriol) and the base (oil of tartar). It was highly valued as a mild laxative and diuretic.</note> be made according to the **Art** <note>The "Art" refers to the traditional methods and skilled practices of alchemy and pharmacy.</note>, and it will be purgative by stool.

The dose is from twelve to thirty drops.

# Antimonial Pills for the Fevers of the aforementioned [Author].

### Chapter XXVIII.

Take **Glass of Antimony** <note>original: "Vetro d’Antimonio." A translucent, glass-like substance created by melting roasted antimony ore; it was a powerful, though often toxic, medicinal base in early modern pharmacy.</note> made with all diligence, and prepared and corrected as will be described below, finely pulverized, half an ounce; **Sugar candy** <note>original: "Zuccaro candido." Large, clear crystals of purified sugar used here as a binding agent and sweetener.</note> three ounces; four ounces of the finest **wheat flour**; and one **scruple** <note>A traditional unit of apothecaries' weight equal to 20 grains, or approximately 1.3 grams.</note> of select **Saffron**. Let it all be cooked with common water over a fire until it reaches the consistency of a firm **polenta** <note>A thick, grain-based porridge.</note>. However, take care that the mass does not burn at the end, but rather remains almost in the form of a loaf. From this, one may form little pills of about half a scruple each and let them dry in a **drying stove** <note>original: "stuffa." A controlled heating chamber used to remove moisture from medicinal preparations without using direct or excessive heat.</note>. These will retain their potency for four years, though their strength will eventually diminish. This remedy is miraculous for uprooting the "mines" <note>The "miniere" or "mines" refers to the hidden source or deep-seated location of a disease within the body.</note> or "ferments" of fever that produce **tertian**, **quartan** <note>Tertian and quartan fevers refer to types of malaria where the fever returns every third or fourth day, respectively.</note>, **continual** <note>original: "finoche," likely a variant of "sinoche" or *synochal* fevers, which were characterized by a constant high temperature without intervals of relief.</note>, and **putrid fevers**, and other similar ailments. They are to be administered when the first sign of the beginning of the **paroxysm** <note>original: "parocismo." The sudden onset or periodic return of a disease's symptoms, such as the shivering and heat of a fever fit.</note> is seen.

The dose is half a scruple in the

...for children, and up to one **dram** <note>A unit of apothecary weight, roughly 3.9 grams.</note> for adults and strong individuals.

# Hartmann's Purging Glass of Antimony <note>Johann Hartmann (1568–1631) was a famous German chemist and physician known for his "iatrochemical" approach, which used chemical and mineral substances for medicine.</note>

### Chapter XXIX.

Take one ounce of pulverized **Glass of Antimony** <note>original: "Vetro d’Antimonio." A substance created by melting antimony ore into a glass-like state; it was a potent emetic and purgative.</note>, and one ounce of **Oil of Vitriol** <note>Concentrated sulfuric acid.</note> or **Oil of Sulfur** made by the bell method <note>A process where sulfur is burned under a glass bell to collect the acidic condensation.</note>. Mix everything carefully inside an earthenware vessel over a slow fire. After it has dried, mix in another ounce of the aforementioned oil, drying it once again, and repeating this operation seven times, adding fresh oil each time.

Then, take some **Tincture of Mastic** <note>A resin from the mastic tree dissolved in alcohol, often used as a binder or to coat pills.</note> extracted with **Spirit of Wine** <note>High-proof alcohol or ethanol.</note>, and place the mixture there to soak for three days. Afterward, set it on fire and let it burn away. The remaining powder should be placed in a **cinder fire** <note>original: "fuoco di cenere." A gentle, steady heat provided by burying the vessel in hot ashes.</note> for twelve hours. Finally, store it for the use mentioned above—that is, for the aforementioned pills.

When this "glass" is administered in doses of three to five **grains** <note>A very small unit of weight, approximately 65 milligrams.</note>, it purges through the bowels.

# On various other authors who have written in favor of Antimony.

### Chapter XXX.

To bring this third book to a close, and to avoid a lengthiness in writing which might bring boredom to the reader, I will only mention a number of several other authors who have written in favor of our **Hero**, or **Champion of Medicine** <note>The author personifies antimony as a heroic figure in the medical world.</note>. I have knowledge of these authors, much to the embarrassment of those few who, based on a vain opinion, reject it as totally useless. Like **heresiarchs** <note>The leaders of a heretical movement or sect.</note>, they have impressed these errors into the minds of those who—failing to consider, as the Italian proverb says, **"from the roof-tiles up"** <note>original: "da' coppi in sù." This idiom suggests people who are superficial or gullible, believing anything they are told without looking for deeper truths or questioning the authorities "above" them.</note>—readily believe everything. They really ought to take example from the fact that, although its use was condemned by public decree in the **Lutetian school** <note>The Medical Faculty of Paris (Lutetia). In 1566, the University of Paris famously banned the use of antimony as a medicine, a decree that sparked a century-long medical conflict.</note> (as **Hoffmann** <note>Likely Caspar Hoffmann (1572–1648), a prominent German physician who documented the medical controversies of his time.</note> notes), an act which seemed destined to deal the final blow to the reputation of

Antimony, and to its followers, if that <note>Referring to the legal ban mentioned on the previous page.</note> had any substance; but as the truth is like oil—which, even if thrown from a height into water, cannot hide itself but immediately floats to the surface—so the truth of the great virtues of **Antimony** has become known. Despite all the contrary factions that stood against it, its most successful use has been fully introduced to conquer malignant and stubborn infirmities, not only in **Paris** and in its schools, but throughout the entire great **Monarchy of France**, and even throughout all of **Europe** with the most fortunate success. Its voice, and that of its followers, despite its detractors, can be said to be more frequent than any other medicine. 

I believe, therefore, that whoever considers and reads of this triumph, honored by so many celebrated and most learned men, will not give credit to the four or five slanderers who have written against it. I will no longer dwell, as I have said, on describing the praises and operations that many other **Chemical** and **Galenic** <note>Followers of Galen, the traditional medical authority; the author notes that even some traditionalists are beginning to accept antimony.</note> authors proclaim—namely, among the Chemists: **Libavius** <note>original: "Libranio." Behold, Reader, the end of this Triumph approaches, since this is the final Book, which is led by *Angelo Sala of Vicenza* <note>Angelo Sala (1576–1637) was a self-taught Italian physician and a significant figure in the history of chemistry. Now, let us leave this aside and return to our subject: our most learned Sala <note>Angelo Sala (1576–1637) was a renowned self-taught physician and chemist. Of him, I shall omit writing what he said in the first two chapters of the *Anatomy of Antimony* <note>original: *Annotomia dell' Antimonio*</note>, since in the first he cites the authorities of the Ancient Authors in favor of **Antimony**, and in the second those of the moderns; for quite enough has been written regarding all of them here previously. What more shall I say? There is nothing so pleasing, sweet, and tempered among those things that serve for our nourishment that, if burned over a period of time, would not offend the brain or induce some other symptom or trouble. One must not, therefore, marvel if a quantity of the vapors of **Antimony**, drawn into the brain, can induce various and strange accidents <term>accidents: *accidenti*, a medical term of the period referring to sudden symptoms or the onset of a morbid condition.</term> in our body, since any vapor of any kind, taken in the same way, can cause diverse evils. We cannot, therefore, say that the vapor of **Antimony** <note>Antimony is a metallic element that was highly controversial in 16th-century medicine; it could be a deadly poison or a potent remedy depending on its preparation.</note> is properly poisonous or **Arsenical**, as some have imagined. He clearly notifies us of this in the third book of *On Long Life* <note>original: *vita longa*</note>, chapter 6, where he discusses the preparation of Antimony with these words: "Take care in the beginning not to corrupt the Antimony; but let it remain entirely whole without losing its form." To these words he adds: "Beneath this is hidden a great secret." For if he had known that Arsenic was present in Antimony, he would have spoken in a different manner; he would have said, before anything else, to separate the Arsenic from it—for he was a great enemy of Arsenic, as he well demonstrates in the first book of *On the Nature of Things* <note>original: *natura delle cose*</note>.

<meta>This page continues the author's warning against the improper preparation of Antimony. The previous page ended mid-sentence, describing how unskilled practitioners inadvertently create toxic compounds instead of helpful medicines.</meta>

...saline and vitriolated <term>vitriolated: *vitriolati*, substances containing vitriol (sulfates), which were often corrosive in concentrated forms.</term> substances, much like **sublimate** <note>original: *solimato*; likely referring to corrosive sublimate (mercury chloride), an extremely toxic compound used in alchemy and medicine.</note> at that stage; it is so corrosive and poisonous in that form that even in a small quantity it can kill, or at least cause the most serious accidents and symptoms. Therefore, everyone must be careful when taking **Antimony** to see from whom and how it was prepared.

The third way in which **Antimony** can cause harm is when it is prepared as an **emetic** <term>emetic: *vomitivo*, a substance used to induce vomiting to clear the stomach.</term> in any form whatsoever, and is used improperly or given in too high a dose. In such cases, by performing a violent **action** <term>action: *Operatione*, the physiological effect or "working" of the medicine within the body.</term>, it evacuates a great quantity of **humors** <term>humors: *humori*, the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed in pre-modern medicine to govern health.</term>—both the good and the bad—and causes a great weakening of strength, and sometimes leads to death in those who are too weak to resist. Therefore, let one be cautious and prudent in its use, and one will never go wrong.

The fourth way that **Antimony** can cause harm—referring specifically to the **emetic**—is that many things must be observed when using it. (I have spoken of this extensively in my ***Guide to Chemistry*** <note>original: *Guida alla Chimica*; this likely refers to a published work by the author, a common practice for 17th-century scientists to cross-reference their own texts.</note>, to which I refer the reader). By not observing these precautions, one can stumble into many dangers. These are all the ways that **Antimony** can be harmful, from which everyone can protect themselves if they use it with caution and obtain it from **skilled masters** <note>original: *periti Maestri*; refers to professionally trained apothecaries or chemists rather than "charlatans."</note>.

Now, regarding other discourses on **Antimony** made by the aforementioned author <note>Referring to Angelo Sala (1576–1637), a physician and chemist who was a key figure in the transition from alchemy to chemistry.</note>, since they are not to our purpose, I will leave them aside, along with certain Operations <term>Operations: *Operationi*, the specific laboratory procedures or chemical transformations used to create a medicine.</term> which, having been described by other authors, I shall omit.

## Concerning Glass of Antimony.

### Chapter II.

Although I have described other recipes for **Glass of Antimony** elsewhere, I nonetheless did not want to fail to include here that of our Sala, as there are many fine observations within it.

**Glass of Antimony** <term>Glass of Antimony: *Vetro d' Antimonio*, a vitreous, translucent substance made by oxidizing antimony and then melting it; it was highly valued as a powerful emetic and purgative.</term> is so called because it has the form, the luster, the resonance, and the fragility representing glass in some way—certainly much better than any other preparation.

It is prepared, then, in various ways; but of all of them, the most excellent and most approved is this one by **Mattioli** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), an influential Italian doctor and botanist.</note>, which he describes in his commentaries on **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, the ancient Greek author of *De Materia Medica*, the foundational text for Western pharmacology.</note> in the chapter on **stibium** <gloss>original: *stimmo*, the ancient term for antimony ore.</gloss> or **Antimony**, which I wished to describe here word for word:

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for creating Glass of Antimony, following the recipe by Mattioli as mentioned on the previous page. The catchword from the previous page was "Piglia" (Take), which matches the opening of this page "Pigliaſi" (Take for yourself).</meta>

Take the highest quality **Antimony** <term>Antimony: *Antimonio*, specifically stibnite (antimony sulfide), a mineral used in early modern medicine for its powerful—and often toxic—emetic effects.</term> which (as **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), the ancient Greek physician whose work remained the standard pharmacological text for over 1,500 years.</note> says) is most splendid like fireflies when broken apart, and is crusty, brittle, and clean of earth and other impurities. Let it be reduced to a powder and placed in an earthenware pan over lit coals to **calcine** <term>Calcine: *calcinare*, to heat a substance to a high temperature in the presence of air to oxidize it or drive off volatile components.</term>. It must be stirred continually with an iron spoon or spatula. 

One must beware of the thin smoke it produces, which stinks like **sulfur** and **orpiment** <term>Orpiment: *orpimento*, a yellow arsenic sulfide mineral; it was known to be highly toxic and shares chemical similarities with antimony.</term>. If inhaled through the mouth, this smoke often causes vomiting; for this reason, one must turn one’s back to the wind. Do not stop moving the mixture, for if you cease, the **Antimony** would burn or liquefy and be reduced to small clumps. However, no matter how much care is taken, it is impossible to prevent this from happening a little bit. When you see this occurring, immediately remove the pan from the fire, separate the hardened clumps from the powder, pulverize them again, and return them once more to be calcined. 

Always keep stirring and continue this operation until it no longer smokes and the **Antimony** has turned into a powder or whitish ash. The sign that it is well-calcined is when, by putting a little of it over lit coals, it does not smoke. Once this is done, take the aforementioned powder—for example, for every half-pound of raw antimony, one ounce—

...half an ounce of **Borax** <term>Borax: *Borace*, a mineral salt used as a flux to lower the melting point of metals and minerals during heating.</term>; the **crude Antimony** and the borax should be reduced to a powder and all placed in a **crucible** <term>Crucible: *crociolo*, a vessel made of heat-resistant material like clay or graphite, used for melting substances at extremely high temperatures.</term>. <meta>This page continues the author’s argument that the dangers of Antimony are not inherent to the substance but are the fault of unskilled practitioners. It concludes the defense against common objections and begins a new section on the specific medicinal benefits of the preparation.</meta>

to drive out the **morbific matter** <term>morbific matter: the "materia morbifica," or the corrupted substance believed by early physicians to be the physical cause of a disease.</term>, or perhaps due to the dosage, they have fallen into such errors, and thus blindly gamble with the lives of men. If, therefore, we wish to abstain from the use of **Antimony** because of some instances of abuse, then for the same reasons we would have to abstain from countless other remedies that are universally judged to be good and not harmful; yet, when they are administered with untimely abuse, we see that they induce lethal and terrible side effects. Therefore, if we wish to speak dispassionately and without any bias, we must not only consider the evils that can occur through its abuse, but also—and more importantly—consider the benefits it can bring through its miraculous effects, and how, when administered with the proper methods and requirements, it will allow us to triumph over rebellious and desperate diseases.

And so we conclude that **Glass of Antimony** cannot cause any harm, and is no more poisonous or ruinous than any other kind of **emetic** <term>emetic: a substance used to induce vomiting, referred to in the original text as a "vomitorio."</term> used by the ancients; this should be enough to refute the aforementioned objections.

### The virtues of Glass of Antimony

He then transitions to listing the specific therapeutic benefits of Glass of Antimony.</summary>

## Method of preparing the Crocus of Metals according to Sala. <note>Angelo Sala (1576–1637) was a prominent physician and chemist who advocated for the use of chemical medicines and was known for his rigorous laboratory methods.</note>

### Chapter III.

The **Crocus of Metals** <term>Crocus of Metals: A chemical preparation of antimony, usually a liver-colored or deep orange powder, used as a powerful medicine.</term> is nothing other than **Antimony** prepared for the same purpose that one usually prepares **Glass of Antimony**. It is, therefore, a shortcut for a lengthy labor, because in a very short time one can prepare a great amount of **Crocus of Metals**—and in much greater quantity—than could be made of Glass in ten days. Regarding why it is called the **Crocus of Metals**, I can offer no other reason than that when it is dissolved in the first water for washing (as will be taught below), it yields a yellow color that stains the hands like **Saffron** <term>Saffron: known as *Zaffrano* in Italian.</term>, which the Latins called **Crocum** <note>original: "Crocum"; the Latin word for saffron. The name "Crocus" was often used in alchemy to describe powders of a deep yellow, orange, or reddish hue.</note>.

<summary>The author explains that the Crocus of Metals is a more efficient alternative to Glass of Antimony, offering the same medicinal benefits with a much shorter preparation time. He notes that the name derives from its saffron-like yellow color that stains the hands during the washing process.</summary>

## Preparation of the **Flowers of Antimony** of various colors.

### Chapter IV.

The **Flowers of Antimony** <note>original: "fiori d'Antimonio"</note> are nothing other than a very fine soot <note>original: "fuligine"</note> which, by sublimating <term>Sublimating: The alchemical process of heating a solid substance until it turns into vapor, which then cools and collects as a solid again, often in the form of a fine powder.</term> the **Antimony** intensely, attaches itself around the sides of the vessels that contain it. This soot is nothing but a sulfurous and mercurial substance driven out by the force of fire in the form of vapors from the remaining body of the same **Antimony**. As for the name, I believe they are called "flowers" for the following three causes: first, that since this is the most subtle and volatile substance within the **Antimony**, they are so named in imitation and analogy to the flowers of trees, which...

Each of these can be kept separate, and they all possess the same virtue <note>"Virtue" refers to the inherent medicinal power or efficacy of the substance</note>, keeping to the same dosage; therefore, many mix them all together.

These flowers are highly esteemed by many physicians, among whom Zapata <note>Giovanni Battista Zapata, an influential 16th-century Italian physician famous for his book of medical "secrets."</note>, a most experienced doctor, makes a great account of them. This is both because they possess more strength and virtue than the **Glass** <term>Vetro d'Antimonio: a glassy, translucent substance made by melting antimony ore; it was a potent but often dangerous emetic</term>, and also because their dose can be more safely administered and measured than that of the Glass. It seems that every time the Glass is prepared, it differs somewhat in its purging power, which does not happen with the flowers. Nevertheless, I believe without a doubt that this variation could still occur with the flowers if, at one time more than another, they are subjected to a fierce fire; in that case, the flowers will have a diaphoretic <term>diaforetica: a substance that induces sweating, often used to "sweat out" a fever</term> virtue rather than a purgative one. 

Be that as it may, I will not deny that the flower of Antimony is a most excellent and distinguished <term>remedio</term> <note>original: "remedio"</note> when taken with the necessary precautions. I remember having known a certain young physician in Geneva called Magno Piamontese, who used these flowers of Antimony to make certain lozenges or tablets in the form described by Zapata. With these, he successfully completed many remarkable cures for ailments resulting from catarrh <note>An excessive buildup of phlegm or mucus, believed in this period to cause many different diseases by "flowing" to different parts of the body</note> and fluxes, and likewise for intermittent fevers, disorders of the stomach <note>original: "Vetricolo," a variant of "ventricolo" used here to refer to the stomach or digestive cavity</note>, melancholy, mania, and various other infirmities.

# Orange-Colored Flowers of Antimony.

### Chapter V.

Take one pound of raw Antimony and six ounces of Sal Ammoniac <term>Sal Armoniaco: ammonium chloride, a salt used in alchemy to aid the sublimation and purification of minerals</term>. Let the whole be pulverized finely and placed inside an earthenware vessel <note>original: "orinale di terra", literally an earthen urinal, a common shape for chemical pots</note> positioned in a furnace of appropriate shape, so that it may be heated <note>original: "colorato", likely referring to the pot becoming red-hot</note>, and let the mouth be a little higher than the bottom. Let it be set within the aforementioned furnace according to the art, with a hole or vent <note>original: "bucco ò registro"</note> in the middle through which the flame may pass out. Then, fit it with a blind cap <term>capello cieco: a large lid or "aludel" head without an outlet, used to catch and condense vapors into solid form</term> that is large. Thus, apply fire to it little by little, by degrees, until it is observed that a certain white soot <note>the condensed vapor, or sublimate</note> begins to sublimate within the said cap, and...

## Antimonial Hippocras.

### Chapter VI.

Take eighty grains of finely pulverized Glass of Antimony <term>Vetro d’Antimonio: a glassy substance created by melting antimony ore; it was used to infuse liquids with emetic properties</term>, and seven and a half ounces of the best white wine. Place everything to digest <note>to steep or soak at a gentle heat</note> in a glass vessel very well sealed, inside a warm bath <note>a "bain-marie" or water bath</note> or upon hot ashes for twenty-four hours. Then, let it be strained, and to the strained liquid add two and a half ounces of finely pulverized fine sugar. Let it be flavored with the most perfect cinnamon water, and let it be preserved for use.

## Another Antimonial Wine.

### Chapter VII.

Take sixty grains of finely pulverized Glass of Antimony, six ounces and two drams of white Spanish Wine <term>Vino di Spagna: likely a fortified wine like Sherry or Sack, common in early pharmacy</term>, one and a half ounces of Syrup of Violets <note>original: "Siropo Violato", a common medicinal syrup used for its color and soothing properties</note>, and six or eight drops of oil of sulfur made by the bell <term>oglio di solfo fatto per campana: a method of producing sulfuric acid by burning sulfur under a glass bell to collect the condensation</term>. Mix everything together and let it sit for a while in digestion <note>steeping at a gentle heat</note>, then filter it through paper. One will then have a purple, fragrant liquid that is very pleasant to the taste. The dosage mentioned above should be followed.

If it is desired that the said dose be in a smaller quantity <note>referring to the concentration of the medicine</note>, let the amount of wine be increased.

## Very Excellent Emetic Oxysaccharum <term>Oxysaccharum: a medicinal syrup made from vinegar and sugar, from the Greek "oxys" (acid) and "saccharon" (sugar)</term>.

->Chapter VIII.<-

Take half an ounce of Glass of Antimony <term>Vetro d’Antimonio: a medicinal substance created by melting antimony ore into a transparent, glass-like state, used to induce vomiting</term>, and eight ounces of good, fragrant white wine vinegar. Place it to infuse in a warm bath <note>original: "bagno tepido," likely a water bath or *bain-marie* used for gentle heating</note> or hot ashes for fifteen hours. Afterward, strain it, and to the strained liquid add four ounces of fine sugar. Reduce it in a glass vessel into the form of a syrup, and keep it for use.

This Oxysaccharum is pleasant to the taste and can be used as an emetic <term>vomitorio: a substance that induces vomiting, used in this period to purge the body of "corrupt" humors</term> against continuous hot, pestilential, and intermittent fevers <note>fevers that come and go, such as malaria</note> shortly before the paroxysm <note>original: "paroſiſmo," the point at which a fever or fit is at its highest intensity</note>. It is also used against poisons and smallpox <term>Varoli: smallpox</term> before they progress, or other ailments where intense heat is joined by putrefaction, obstruction, and an abundance of viscous humors. This is because of the vinegar mixed within, which tempers the heat and the effervescence, or boiling <note>in early modern medicine, fever was often conceptualized as a literal "boiling" or fermentation of the blood</note>.

<summary>This page concludes a recipe for antimonial wine and introduces Chapter VIII, a recipe for an emetic syrup made from antimony-infused vinegar and sugar (Oxysaccharum). This remedy was intended to treat fevers, poisons, and smallpox by cooling the "boiling" humors of the body.</summary>

...of the blood, resists corruption, and through its incisive <note>original: "incisoria," referring to the medicine's ability to "cut" through or break up thick, sticky substances in the body</note> power, it thins and detaches phlegmatic and viscous humors. In particular, it acts as a vehicle, a restorative, and a corrective for the Antimony. Therefore, any prudent Physician may use it very successfully in many other illnesses. The dose of the aforementioned [syrup] is from two to six drams <term>drame: a dram, a unit of apothecaries' weight approximately equal to 3.9 grams</term>.

## Pearled Emetic Tablets

->Chapter X.<-

Take seven scruples of prepared pearls <note>original: "Perle preparate." In early modern pharmacy, pearls were often "prepared" by grinding them into a fine powder or dissolving them in acidic juice to make them digestible</note>, three scruples of white Flowers of Antimony, and twenty scruples of fine pulverized sugar. Make a mass using tragacanth <term>dragante: a natural gum derived from the sap of legumes, used as a binding agent for pills and tablets</term> dissolved in rose water, and scent it with a little Oil of Cinnamon. Form small or thin tablets; once dry, the dose shall be from one scruple up to...

We should not go seeking novelties in **Antimony**, but leave those to be sought by the sophisticated inventors of uncertain things.

# The reason for preparing Antimony so that it purges through sweats and insensible perspiration.

### Chapter XI.

Although it will perhaps seem like an incredible thing to some that **Antimony** possesses a certain substance that makes it **sudorific** <term>sudorifico: a substance that induces sweating</term>, and causes it to act through **insensible perspiration** <term>insensibile transpiratione: the release of moisture or waste from the body through the skin that is not visible as liquid sweat</term>—especially since such a great **emetic** <term>emetica: a substance that induces vomiting</term> force predominates in it, which is not separated from it even by the force of fire, however violent, as is seen in the preparation of **Antimonial Glass** <term>Vetro Antimoniale: a reddish-brown, transparent glass-like substance made by roasting and melting antimony ore, often used to induce vomiting</term>—nevertheless, for those who have some knowledge of the secrets of nature and who are **adepts** <term>adepti: individuals initiated into the secrets of alchemy or hermetic philosophy</term>, it will not be difficult to understand it. And so that we may show how this is possible, it is enough to place before our eyes two principal heads, namely: the substantial composition of things, and the power of the **Chemical Art** <term>Arte Chimica: the practice of alchemy and chemistry, particularly the "spagyric" process of separating and purifying substances</term> regarding that which at fir-

<summary>This chapter introduces the counter-intuitive idea that Antimony, typically known for causing violent vomiting, can be processed through alchemy to act as a gentle medicine that promotes sweating and perspiration.</summary>
<keywords>Antimony, sudorific, insensible perspiration, emetic, Antimonial Glass, adepts, Chemical Art, alchemy, Spagyric medicine</keywords>

--- Page 328 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 262, completing the phrase "first head" (primo capo). The author is explaining how complex substances are made of various components that can be separated and transformed through alchemy.</meta>

<page-num>263</page-num>

**first head** belongs, it is a thing clear as the light of midday that all creatures consist of diverse substances, different among themselves in form, quality, property, and virtue. And although, while remaining united in one body, it happens that the most powerful among them has dominion over the others in such a way that it hides and weakens them; or they act, while so united, in a very different way than they do when they are separated, each being disunited from the other.

To anyone who does not know this, it is only necessary to show them the **chemical operation** <term>operatione chimica: a laboratory process, such as distillation or sublimation, used to separate and purify the components of a substance</term> which is performed upon **wine**—a liquid well known and used among men. First, one will see the separation of a very subtle, thin, and incorruptible essence, clear and shining like a crystal; it is not oily, and yet it is more combustible than any oil. This essence is called by the common people **water of life** <note>original: *aquavita*</note> or **burning water** <note>original: *acqua ardente*</note>, **Spirit of wine** <term>Spirito di vino: ethanol or high-proof alcohol obtained through distillation</term>, and **celestial sulfur** <term>solfo celeste: an alchemical term for the volatile, flammable "soul" or active principle of a substance</term>. 

After this, one sees a great quantity of cold and tasteless water, subject to corruption just like common water. After said water follows a **Spirit in smoke**, which is called **Spirit of Tartar** <term>Spirito di Tartaro: a liquid produced by the dry distillation of cream of tartar or wine lees, often used in historical medicine to induce sweating</term>, which is a very **sudorific** <note>inducing sweat</note> substance. Shortly after this, there follows a fat and greasy **Oil** <note>original: *Oglio*</note>, thick and combustible.

<summary>The author uses the distillation of wine as a primary example to show how a single substance contains multiple components (alcohol, water, spirit of tartar, and oil) with vastly different properties. This serves as an analogy for how the "emetic" or vomit-inducing nature of Antimony might be separated from its "sudorific" or healing nature.</summary>

**Galen** <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–c. 216 AD) was the most influential physician of the Roman Empire; the author cites him here to give "chemical" medicine traditional legitimacy</note>, Prince of Rational Physicians, in various places in the books he wrote on the *Power of Simple Medicines*, attests that not only are earthly creatures composed of a diversity of substances (even though the method of separating them was unknown to him), but that for this reason, even one and the same thing can act in diverse ways. Among the many assertions he makes about this in the aforementioned books—which I omit for the sake of brevity—I will include here only the one found in the ninth book of the said works, where he speaks of **Vitriol** <term>Vitriolo: a metal sulfate (like iron or copper sulfate) known for its sharp, acidic, and drying qualities</term> with the following words: *“Furthermore, one is moved to wonder about this medicine, in what way the most vehement **astringency** <note>original: "adstrictioni," referring to the power to contract or tighten body tissues</note>...”*

## Sudorific Antimony, or Diaphoretic
<note>Diaphoretic is another term for a substance that induces sweating, often used to break a fever</note>

### Chapter XII.

Take the best **Antimony**, and refined **Saltpeter** <term>Saltpeter: also known as niter (potassium nitrate), used in alchemy as a powerful oxidizing agent to help purify or react with minerals</term>, one pound of each. Once the whole is reduced to powder, it is calcined <note>Heated to a high temperature to drive off volatile substances or to oxidize it</note> in the manner that was described for making the **Crocus of Metals** <term>Crocus of Metals: a reddish-brown powder, usually an oxide, created by heating a metal; named "Crocus" due to its resemblance to the color of the saffron flower</term>. Then, having been "sweetened" <note>The process of "sweetening" (edulcoration) involved washing the calcined powder with water to remove any leftover caustic or bitter salts</note> once more, it is joined with double its weight of saltpeter as above. Once pulverized, let it be placed in a large **crucible** <term>Crucible: a ceramic or metal container capable of withstanding the extreme heat of a furnace</term> inside a **wind furnace** <note>A furnace designed to draw in a strong natural draft of air to reach the high temperatures necessary for melting minerals</note> with glowing coals so that the Antimony and Saltpeter melt together, and let it be left thus in fu-

<meta>catchword: fuf- (fusion)</meta>

...fusion for at least two hours, occasionally throwing some lit coals into the crucible to burn the **Saltpeter** <term>Saltpeter: potassium nitrate, used here as an oxidizing agent to purify the mineral</term>. Afterward, let the said water settle, and once it is clear, pour it off by tilting the vessel. ### Explanation of the Image which demonstrates in Hieroglyphic form the operation of the Philosopher’s Stone.

The old man sitting with an anvil at his side and a bellows in his hands signifies Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire and smithing, here representing the alchemical fire and the controlled heat of the furnace.</note>—that is, the fire. The tree that stands at the foot of the mountain, beneath which rises a clear spring, and at the side of which is a <term>sagiolo: A small glass vessel or phial used for testing and assaying substances; from the Italian "saggio" (test/sample).</term> (a chemical glass); the Spring signifies the matter of the stone, and the *sagiolo* signifies the vessel where the work is performed by means of fire. This is intended to imply that with only one single fire, one single vessel, and one single matter, the Philosopher’s Stone must be crafted; and no one should ever set themselves to such a work until they know upon which matter they must labor.

At the beginning of the ascent of the Mountain, there is an old man who is in the act of devouring an armed youth; from both of them issue rays that form a starry globe. The old man is Saturn <note>In alchemical symbolism, Saturn represents Lead or the "nigredo" (blackening) stage. The image of Saturn devouring his child is a classical myth often used to represent the process of dissolution or the "fixed" matter consuming the "volatile."</note>, 

<vocab>Hieroglyph, Philosopher's Stone, Vulcan, Chemical Glass, Assay Vessel, Saturn, Alchemy</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the symbolic figures in an alchemical illustration, identifying Vulcan as fire and a spring as the primary "matter," while emphasizing that the Great Work requires only one vessel, one fire, and one material. It also introduces the figure of Saturn devouring a youth as a symbol for the initial stages of the chemical process.</summary>

The Youth sitting on the side of the road, and Mercury <note>The messenger god, representing the "volatile" principle or the "Sophic Mercury" essential to the work.</note>, who attentively gazes at the aforementioned starry Globe, which is surrounded by nine Eagles <note>In alchemical imagery, eagles represent the process of sublimation—the act of a substance "taking flight" as it is heated and purified.</note>, and above him fly two doves, which emerge from the grove of Venus; and this signifies the preparation of the matter through which the Mercury of the Philosophers <term>Mercury of the Philosophers: Also called "Sophic Mercury," this is a specialized substance prepared by the alchemist, distinct from common quicksilver, used as the "seed" of the Stone.</term> is made, signified by the hermaphrodite—that is, that figure which has two heads, one of a Male and the other of a Female—surrounded by Planets.

And here begins the operation of the Stone, with the Old Man with the scythe in hand signifying the reign of Saturn <note>The "regimen of Saturn" is the initial stage of the Great Work, characterized by decay and the color black (nigredo).</note>, who further ahead is seen languishing and yielding the scepter to Jove <note>Jupiter. This signifies the second stage of the process, where the blackness begins to give way to a gray or variegated color palette.</note>, who is the one crowned with the Eagle at his side and the lightning bolts in his hand; and this is called the reign of Jove, who at the end yields the scepter to the Moon <note>The Moon represents the stage of Albedo, or "The White Work," where the matter reaches a state of crystalline purity.</note>, who is that woman riding the Dragon, and thus her reign begins. 

When this is finished, she distributes her kingdom to Saturn, Jove, Mercury, Mars, and Venus, signifying by this that the matter has been perfected to the white <term>materia al bianco: The "White Work" or Albedo; at this stage, the alchemist has created a substance capable of transmuting base metals into silver.</term>. Now, the reign of the Moon having finished, wish... <note>original: "volendo"</note>

excellent intellect, and also endowed with very beautiful virtues, beyond Painting; that is, with fine Literature, Poetry, and other Sciences. And thus, the aforementioned Signor Mario <note>Alessandro Mario Piamontese, the artist mentioned on the previous page.</note>, having understood from me the significance of the Operation displayed in the aforementioned Hieroglyphic <note>A "hieroglyphic" in this context is a complex symbolic diagram used in alchemy to represent the chemical and spiritual stages of the Great Work.</note>, immediately took pen in hand, and spontaneously explained in a Sonnet so eruditly the entire Operation—as much as could be done with a very long Discourse. Seeing this, I took it from him against his will, while he insisted that I return it to him so that he might improve it; but because it pleased me, I wished to preserve it as it was, and on this occasion I have set it out here, so that it might be easier for the devoted student to prepare for the Work.

<meta>This page completes the sonnet by the artist Alessandro Mario Piamontese and provides the author's concluding remarks. It transitions from the poetic interpretation of the "Hieroglyphic" to a final exhortation to the reader, referencing the "Triumphal Chariot of Antimony."</meta>

Give the Scepter to Jupiter <note>In the alchemical "Great Work," Jupiter represents the metal tin or the transition from the blackness of lead to the whiteness of silver.</note>, and he, on the other side,
Will yield it to the Moon <note>The Moon (Luna) symbolizes silver or the *Albedo* (the whitening stage of the process).</note> with better fortune.
Here Venus <note>Venus represents the metal copper.</note> shall have a better Guide
Behind Mars <note>Mars represents iron; the sequence suggests the purification of metals toward perfection.</note>, who will go forth proud and content,
Where the Sun <note>The Sun (Sol) symbolizes gold or the final *Rubedo* (redness), the completion of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> gives the most beautiful splendor to all.
You will not be a wanderer (say Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of the Hermetic arts.</note> and Lully <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a major figure in medieval logic and alchemy.</note>),
Wherefore, to make the heart happy and glad,
May the Tagus <note>The Tagus is a river in Spain and Portugal once famous for its gold-bearing sands; here it serves as a metaphor for achieving wealth and the "Golden" result of the work.</note> grant you its golden sands.

Now, with this Hieroglyphic <note>A "hieroglyphic" refers to the symbolic diagram or image that this text is interpreting.</note> and many other particulars that the devoted student may draw from this *Triumphal Chariot of Antimony* <note>original: "Trionfo dell' Antimonio." This likely refers to the influential alchemical text *Currus Triumphalis Antimonii*, traditionally attributed to the monk Basil Valentine.</note>, and also from many authorities cited in the *Triumphal Chariot of Mercury*, he will be able—I say—to facilitate his understanding of the books of the Philosophers <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers specifically to alchemists, often called "Hermetic Philosophers."</note>. Therefore, Reader, whoever you may be, welcome this good intention; and by sympathizing with my weakness, you will oblige me to pray to His Divine Majesty <note>original: "S. D. M." (Sua Divina Maestà).</note> that He may give you His holy grace and happy health in this life, and Glory in the next, which may it please Him to grant to us all. And so be it.

->PRAISE BE TO GOD.<-

The author cites the influence of Basil Valentine's "Triumphal Chariot of Antimony" as a key to understanding Hermetic philosophy.</summary>
